The wild geometric Langlands correspondence also has a categorical formulation: the category of D-modules on Bun_G(X) with wild ramification is equivalent to the category of modules over the wild W-algebra, which is the quantum Drinfeld-Sokolov reduction of the affine Kac-Moody algebra at a non-critical level with a non-principal nilpotent element. The wild Verlinde formula computes the dimension of the space of wild conformal blocks as the quantum dimension of the corresponding representation of the quantum group at a root of unity.

The Categorical Trace and Character Theory for D-modules on Loop Groups

The categorical trace, or Hochschild homology, of a monoidal category provides a powerful invariant that categorifies the character theory of groups and quantum groups. For the category D(Gr_G) of D-modules on the affine Grassmannian Gr_G = G((t))/G[[t]], the categorical trace Tr(D(Gr_G)) is equivalent to the category of D-modules on the loop group LG = G((t)) equipped with the adjoint action of G[[t]]. This equivalence, established by Ben-Zvi, Francis, and Nadler, is a geometric realization of the fact that the trace of the representation category of a group is the category of representations of the loop group. The categorical trace carries a natural S1-action (the circle action coming from the rotation of the loop), and the S1-equivariant categorical trace computes the cyclic homology. For the spherical Hecke category Sph_G = DG[[t]](Gr_G), the categorical trace is equivalent to the category of D-modules on the adjoint quotient LG/Ad G[[t]], which by the geometric Satake correspondence is equivalent to the category of representations of the Langlands dual group ^L G. The character theory of D-modules on the loop group is developed via the theory of character D-modules: for a D-module M on LG, its character is the distribution on the Lie algebra Lg = g((t)) obtained by taking the trace of the monodromy of M along loops. The character D-module construction associates to a representation V of the affine Kac-Moody algebra ĝ at level k a D-module Ch(V) on the loop group whose categorical trace recovers the character of V as a distribution on the Cartan subalgebra. The Weyl-Kac character formula is realized geometrically as the localization formula for the categorical trace on the loop group: the character of an integrable highest weight representation is the equivariant index of a certain D-module on the affine flag variety. The categorical trace also provides a geometric construction of the Verlinde algebra: the fusion product on the category of D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles on a curve X induces a convolution product on the categorical trace, which is the Verlinde algebra. For a modular tensor category C, the categorical trace of the Drinfeld center Z(C) is equivalent to the category of boundary conditions in the associated 3-dimensional topological quantum field theory. The character theory for D-modules on the loop group extends to the quantum setting: for q a root of unity, the categorical trace of the category of D-modules on the quantum affine Grassmannian is equivalent to the category of representations of the quantum loop group U_q(Lg). The categorical trace of the category of D-modules on the moduli stack of G-local systems on a surface Σ computes the space of states of the associated 4-dimensional topological quantum field theory on Σ × S1.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles on Higher Dimensional Varieties and the Higher Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The geometric Langlands correspondence has been extended from curves to higher dimensional varieties by Gaitsgory, Rozenblyum, and others, replacing the moduli stack of G-bundles on a curve with the moduli stack of G-bundles on a smooth projective variety X of dimension d ≥ 2. The automorphic category is the category of D-modules on Bun_G(X), which is an algebraic stack locally of finite type but not of finite type. The spectral side is the category of ind-coherent sheaves on the moduli stack LocSys^L G(X) of ^L G-local systems on X. The higher geometric Langlands conjecture asserts an equivalence between these categories, compatible with the six-functor formalism. The key new feature in higher dimensions is the appearance of higher categorical structures: the category of D-modules on Bun_G(X) carries an action of the higher Hecke category, which is the category of D-modules on the higher Hecke stack Hecke_X = { (E, E', x) | E, E' ∈ Bun_G(X), x ∈ X, E|_{X{x}} ≃ E'|_{X{x}} }. The higher Hecke category is a monoidal (∞, d)-category, and the automorphic category is a module over it. The spectral side carries an action of the category of ind-coherent sheaves on the stack of ^L G-local systems on the Ran space Ran(X), which is the higher categorical analog of the Hecke category. The higher geometric Langlands correspondence for GL_n on a surface X has been proved by Gaitsgory and Rozenblyum using the theory of D-modules on the moduli stack of Higgs bundles and the non-abelian Hodge correspondence for surfaces. The proof uses the decomposition theorem for the higher Hitchin map h: HiggsGL_n(X) → A_X, where A_X is the space of higher Hitchin Hamiltonians. The fibers of the higher Hitchin map are higher spectral varieties, which are Lagrangian subvarieties of the cotangent bundle of BunGL_n(X). The higher geometric Langlands correspondence also has a categorical formulation: the category of D-modules on Bun_G(X) is equivalent to the category of modules over the higher W-algebra, which is the quantum Drinfeld-Sokolov reduction of the higher affine Kac-Moody algebra associated to the variety X. The higher Verlinde formula computes the Euler characteristic of the automorphic category as the dimension of the space of higher conformal blocks, which is given by the quantum dimension of the corresponding representation of the higher quantum group. The higher geometric Langlands correspondence for non-simply connected groups involves the component group of Bun_G(X), which is isomorphic to H2(X, π1(G)) ⊕ H1(X, π1(G)) ⊕ π1(G). The oper locus in LocSys^L G(X) lies in a specific component determined by the characteristic classes of the local system. The higher geometric Langlands correspondence at the critical level is related to the classical geometric Langlands correspondence on curves via the dimensional reduction: the category of D-modules on Bun_G(X × C) for a curve C is equivalent to the category of D-modules on the moduli stack of G-bundles on C with values in the category of D-modules on X.

  • Confusing the p-adic Riemann-Hilbert correspondence for regular singularities with the irregular correspondence; the latter requires enhanced ind-sheaves and a ramification filtration on the Berkovich space.
  • Assuming the factorization algebra on a nodal curve glues simply as a tensor product over the vacuum space; the correct gluing uses the Zhu algebra action and the regular representation for the node.
  • Neglecting the Stokes filtration on the spectral side of the wild geometric Langlands correspondence; the equivalence must respect the Stokes data, not just the underlying local system.
  • Treating the categorical trace of D(Gr_G) as the ordinary trace of the Grothendieck group; the categorical trace is a category, not a vector space, and its decategorification gives the character ring.
  • Assuming the higher geometric Langlands correspondence for surfaces is a straightforward generalization of the curve case; the higher Hecke category is an (∞, 2)-category, and the automorphic category is a module 2-category, requiring higher categorical machinery.
  • Overlooking the role of the component group of Bun_G(X) for non-simply connected G in higher dimensions; the oper locus lies in a component determined by H2(X, π1(G)), not just π1(G).
  • Misidentifying the quantum parameter in the wild quantum geometric Langlands correspondence; the parameter q is related to the irregularity exponent, not just the level of the affine Kac-Moody algebra.

D-modules on Derived Stacks and the Derived Riemann–Hilbert Correspondence

The classical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence equates the category of regular holonomic D‑modules on a smooth algebraic variety X with the category of constructible sheaves on the associated analytic space Xan. In derived algebraic geometry one replaces X by a derived stack 𝔛 locally of finite presentation over a field k of characteristic zero. The ring of differential operators is replaced by the sheaf of derived differential operators D𝔛, defined as the enveloping algebra of the tangent complex T𝔛 in the symmetric monoidal ∞‑category of quasi‑coherent complexes on 𝔛. A derived D‑module is a module over D𝔛 in this ∞‑category. The derived de Rham functor DR𝔛 sends a derived D‑module M to the derived tensor product M ⊗D𝔛 O𝔛, which lands in the ∞‑category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the underlying classical stack. Gaitsgory and Rozenblyum (2015) proved that for a derived stack 𝔛 which is locally of finite presentation and admits a cotangent complex of Tor‑amplitude ≤ 1, the derived de Rham functor induces an equivalence between the ∞‑category of holonomic derived D‑modules on 𝔛 and the ∞‑category of constructible ind‑coherent sheaves on the associated analytic derived stack 𝔛an. This derived Riemann–Hilbert correspondence respects the six‑functor formalism: the derived pushforward, pullback, and duality for D‑modules correspond under DR to the analogous operations for constructible sheaves. A key technical input is the derived Kashiwara constructibility theorem, which asserts that the derived de Rham complex of a holonomic derived D‑module has constructible cohomology sheaves. The derived correspondence also underlies the derived geometric Satake equivalence, where the derived category of D‑modules on the derived affine Grassmannian GrG is identified with the derived category of representations of the Langlands dual group LG. In applications to the geometric Langlands program, the derived Riemann–Hilbert correspondence allows one to transfer the action of the Hecke category on D‑modules on BunG to an action of the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on LocSysLG on the spectral side, even when BunG is a derived stack with non‑trivial derived structure at singular points of the moduli space.

Opers, Miura Opers, and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Opers

An oper for a reductive group G on a smooth curve X is a G‑connection ∇ on the trivial G‑bundle together with a reduction to a Borel subgroup B ⊂ G satisfying a transversality condition: the induced map on the associated graded of the B‑filtration is an isomorphism. Concretely, for G = SLn an oper is a connection of the form ∇ = d + (p1(z)E1 + … + pn‑1(z)En‑1)dz where Ei are the simple root generators and the pi are differential polynomials in a single function. The moduli stack OpG(X) of G‑opers is a finite‑dimensional algebraic variety, isomorphic to an affine space of dimension (g‑1)dim G + rank G. A Miura oper is an oper equipped with a further reduction to the Cartan subgroup T ⊂ B; the space of Miura opers MiuraG(X) is a torsor over OpG(X) under the action of the group of T‑valued functions. The geometric Langlands correspondence for opers, formulated by Beilinson and Drinfeld and proved by Frenkel–Gaitsgory (2006) for simply‑connected G, states that the category of D‑modules on OpG(X) is equivalent to the category of quasi‑coherent sheaves on the oper locus inside the moduli stack LocSysLG(X) of LG‑local systems. The oper locus is the closed substack of local systems that admit an oper structure; it is a Lagrangian subvariety with respect to the natural symplectic form on LocSysLG(X). The equivalence is realized by the quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction: the category of D‑modules on OpG(X) is identified with the category of modules over the W‑algebra Wk(LG) at the critical level k = −h∨, and the Feigin–Frenkel isomorphism identifies the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level with functions on OpLG(X). This provides a concrete bridge between the automorphic side (D‑modules on BunG) and the spectral side (quasi‑coherent sheaves on LocSysLG) via the oper locus. For non‑simply‑connected groups the oper locus splits into components indexed by H2(X, π1(G)), and the correspondence must be formulated component‑wise, as shown by Ben‑Zvi and Nadler (2018).

Technique: Identifying the oper locus as a Lagrangian subvariety
Explain why the oper locus in LocSysLG(X) is a Lagrangian subvariety with respect to the natural symplectic form on the moduli stack of LG‑local systems.
The moduli stack LocSysLG(X) carries a symplectic form obtained from the cup product pairing on H1(X, ad P) for a local system P. The tangent space at a point corresponding to a local system ∇ is H1(X, ad ∇). The oper locus consists of local systems that admit a reduction to a Borel subgroup with a transverse connection. The condition of being an oper imposes exactly half the number of independent constraints on the connection form: the oper equations are a system of first‑order differential equations whose linearization cuts out a subspace of H1(X, ad ∇) of dimension equal to half the dimension of the full tangent space. Moreover, the symplectic form vanishes on this subspace because the oper equations are derived from a Hamiltonian reduction (the quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction) which produces a coisotropic subvariety; the oper locus is the zero‑level set of the moment map for the action of the nilpotent radical of the Borel, making it Lagrangian. This Lagrangian property is essential for the geometric Langlands correspondence, as it ensures that the category of D‑modules on the oper locus is a module category over the Hecke category and matches the spectral side.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the relationship between opers and the geometric Langlands correspondence, first recall the definition of an oper as a connection with a full flag and transversality. Then state the Beilinson–Drinfeld conjecture and the Frenkel–Gaitsgory theorem: D‑modules on OpG are equivalent to quasi‑coherent sheaves on the oper locus in LocSysLG. Mention the quantum Drinfeld–Sokolov reduction and the Feigin–Frenkel center. If the group is non‑simply‑connected, note the component decomposition by H2(X, π1(G)). This structured answer covers definition, theorem, mechanism, and subtlety.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of Connections with Irregular Singularities and the Wild Character Variety

Let X be a smooth projective curve and D ⊂ X a reduced divisor. A meromorphic connection with irregular singularities along D is a vector bundle E on X equipped with a connection ∇ : E → E ⊗ ΩX(*D) whose formal type at each point of D has non‑zero irregular part. The moduli stack ConnG(X, D) of G‑connections with prescribed irregular type is an algebraic stack locally of finite type. Its coarse moduli space, the wild character variety, parametrizes isomorphism classes of such connections modulo gauge transformations that are the identity on the formal punctured discs. Boalch (2001) constructed the wild character variety as a symplectic algebraic variety obtained by Hamiltonian reduction of a product of Stokes‑graded local systems. The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence, developed by Sabbah, Mochizuki, and Kedlaya, relates the category of holonomic D‑modules on X with irregular singularities along D to the category of Stokes‑filtered local systems on X D, or equivalently to enhanced ind‑sheaves on the real blow‑up of X along D. In the language of D‑modules, one considers the sheaf of differential operators DX(*D) with poles along D and studies holonomic modules over it. The Stokes filtration on the de Rham complex of such a D‑module encodes the asymptotic behavior of solutions; the associated graded pieces are regular holonomic D‑modules on the real blow‑up. The wild geometric Langlands correspondence, conjectured by Frenkel–Gross and formulated categorically by Gaitsgory–Raskin, predicts an equivalence between the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack of G‑connections with irregular singularities and the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the wild Langlands dual stack, which is built from the wild character variety of LG‑connections. A crucial feature is that the equivalence must respect the Stokes data: the Stokes matrices become part of the spectral data, and the categorical equivalence intertwines the Stokes filtration on the automorphic side with a natural filtration on the spectral side coming from the stratification of the wild character variety by Stokes type. Recent work of D. Arinkin and R. Bezrukavnikov (2020) established a version of this equivalence for GLn on the projective line with one irregular singularity using the theory of categorical traces and the affine Grassmannian.

The Theory of Chiral Differential Operators and Vertex Algebras from D-modules

Chiral differential operators (CDOs) on a smooth curve X are a sheaf of vertex algebras DXch that quantize the classical algebra of differential operators on the loop space LX. They were introduced by Malikov, Schechtman, and Vaintrob (1999) and later reinterpreted by Beilinson and Drinfeld as the chiral algebra associated to the D‑module of delta‑functions on the moduli stack BunG(X). Concretely, for a vector bundle V on X, the CDO DVch is a sheaf of vertex algebras whose space of global sections carries the structure of a vertex algebra isomorphic to the βγ‑system of rank rank V. The construction proceeds by taking the D‑module of distributions on the jet scheme J∞V and applying the chiral de Rham functor. The resulting vertex algebra has a conformal vector of central charge c = −2 rank V. Beilinson and Drinfeld showed that the category of modules over DXch is equivalent to the category of D‑modules on the loop space LX that are equivariant with respect to the loop rotation action. This equivalence is a key ingredient in the geometric Langlands correspondence at the critical level: the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level acts on the category of D‑modules on BunG, and this action factors through the algebra of functions on OpLG(X). The chiral algebra of CDOs on the affine Grassmannian GrG provides a realization of the Feigin–Frenkel center. Moreover, the chiral homology of the CDO on X computes the space of conformal blocks for the associated W‑algebra. In the work of Frenkel and Ben‑Zvi (2004), the chiral de Rham complex on a smooth variety is constructed as a sheaf of vertex algebras whose cohomology yields the elliptic genus. The theory of CDOs thus bridges D‑module theory, vertex algebras, and the geometric Langlands program, providing a concrete vertex‑algebraic model for the critical‑level center and for the Hecke eigensheaf property.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of Higgs Bundles and the Higher Rank Hitchin Fibration

The Hitchin fibration for a reductive group G on a smooth projective curve X is the proper map h : HiggsG(X) → AG(X) from the moduli stack of G‑Higgs bundles (pairs (E, φ) with E a G‑bundle and φ a section of ad(E) ⊗ KX) to the affine space of invariant polynomials on the Lie algebra, AG(X) = ⊕i=1r H0(X, KXdi), where di are the degrees of fundamental invariants. The generic fiber is an abelian variety — the Prym variety of the spectral curve Σa ⊂ T*X defined by the characteristic polynomial of φ. Ngô’s proof of the fundamental lemma (2010) uses the decomposition theorem for the Hitchin map: the direct image complex Rh*ℚHiggs splits into a direct sum of shifted perverse sheaves, each supported on a stratum of AG corresponding to the singularity type of the spectral curve. In the D‑module setting, one considers the category of holonomic D‑modules on HiggsG(X) and the pushforward along h. The non‑abelian Hodge correspondence of Simpson (1992) provides a homeomorphism between the moduli space of semistable Higgs bundles and the moduli space of semisimple local systems, which is a diffeomorphism but not holomorphic. This correspondence lifts to an equivalence between the category of regular holonomic D‑modules on the Higgs moduli stack (with appropriate support conditions) and the category of constructible sheaves on the character variety. The higher rank Hitchin fibration for G = GLn has been used by Kapustin and Witten (2007) to give a physical interpretation of the geometric Langlands correspondence via S‑duality of N=4 super‑Yang–Mills theory. The D‑modules on HiggsG(X) that are Hecke eigensheaves correspond under non‑abelian Hodge to D‑modules on the moduli stack of local systems that are eigen for the spectral Hecke operators. Recent work of Chaudouard and Laumon (2016) extends the decomposition theorem to the case of parabolic Higgs bundles, and the theory of D‑modules on the parabolic Hitchin fibration underlies the parabolic geometric Langlands correspondence. The categorical version, due to Arinkin and Gaitsgory, identifies the category of D‑modules on HiggsG(X) with the category of modules over the quantum Hitchin algebra, which is a deformation of the algebra of functions on the Hitchin base.

  • Confusing the derived Riemann–Hilbert correspondence with the classical one; the derived version works for derived stacks and uses ind‑coherent sheaves, not ordinary constructible sheaves.
  • Assuming the oper locus is always a single connected component; for non‑simply‑connected groups it splits according to H2(X, π1(G)).
  • Neglecting the Stokes filtration when stating the irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence; the equivalence is between Stokes‑filtered local systems and enhanced ind‑sheaves, not plain local systems.
  • Treating chiral differential operators as ordinary D‑modules on the loop space; they are vertex algebras, and their module categories are module categories over a vertex algebra, not just D‑module categories.
  • Overlooking the role of the decomposition theorem in the Hitchin fibration; the direct image of the constant sheaf (or D‑module) splits into perverse summands indexed by the singularity type of the spectral curve.
  • Misidentifying the quantum parameter in the wild geometric Langlands correspondence; the parameter is the irregularity exponent, not the level of the affine Kac–Moody algebra.
  • Assuming the non‑abelian Hodge correspondence is an equivalence of algebraic categories; it is a homeomorphism of underlying analytic spaces, and the categorical equivalence requires the theory of twistor D‑modules.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles for Non-Reductive Groups and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The classical geometric Langlands correspondence is formulated for a reductive algebraic group G over a smooth projective curve X. When G is non-reductive, the moduli stack Bun_G of G-bundles on X is no longer an algebraic stack of finite type; it is an ind-stack whose connected components are infinite-dimensional. The ring of differential operators DBun_G must be defined in the ind-setting, and the category of holonomic D-modules is replaced by a category of ind-holonomic D-modules equipped with a renormalization procedure to control infinite rank. The Langlands dual group ^L G of a non-reductive group is a semi-direct product of the dual of the reductive quotient G/U (where U is the unipotent radical) with a vector group dual to the Lie algebra of U. This dual group is not reductive, so the moduli stack of ^L G-local systems LocSys^L G is also an ind-stack. The expected equivalence relates the category of ind-holonomic D-modules on Bun_G to the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on LocSys^L G that are constructible with respect to a suitable stratification. A major technical obstacle is the failure of properness for the Hitchin map in the non-reductive setting, which prevents a direct application of the decomposition theorem. Recent approaches use the theory of categorical traces and the geometry of the affine Grassmannian for the reductive quotient to define a renormalized category of D-modules that admits a well-behaved Hecke action. The resulting correspondence is expected to recover the classical geometric Langlands correspondence for the reductive quotient and to encode additional data coming from the unipotent radical, such as extensions of local systems by vector bundles.

In practice, constructing the functor from D-modules on Bun_G to sheaves on LocSys^L G requires a version of the Whittaker normalization for non-reductive groups. The Whittaker category is defined using a non-degenerate character of the unipotent radical, and the geometric Satake equivalence for the reductive quotient is extended by a convolution with the category of representations of the vector group. This yields a monoidal category of Hecke operators that acts on the category of D-modules on Bun_G. The eigenobjects for this action are precisely the D-modules that correspond to ^L G-local systems. The theory is still under development, but it provides a framework for understanding geometric Langlands for groups such as the Borel subgroup of a reductive group or the group of upper triangular matrices.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles for Supergroups and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

A supergroup is a group object in the category of supermanifolds, equivalently a Z/2-graded Lie group whose even part is a reductive algebraic group and whose odd part is a representation of the even part. The moduli stack Bun_G of principal G-bundles on a curve X is a superstack, and the sheaf of differential operators DBun_G is a sheaf of superalgebras. Holonomic D-modules in this context are defined using the super version of the characteristic variety, which lives in the odd cotangent bundle. The geometric Langlands correspondence for supergroups predicts an equivalence between the category of holonomic D-modules on Bun_G and the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack of local systems for the Langlands dual supergroup ^L G. The dual supergroup has the same even part as the dual of the even part of G, but its odd part is the dual representation. This correspondence is closely related to the representation theory of quantum supergroups at the critical level, where the Feigin-Frenkel center is replaced by a super version of the center of the affine Kac-Moody superalgebra. The chiral algebra of chiral differential operators on the super affine Grassmannian provides a vertex superalgebra realization of this center.

One of the main new phenomena is the appearance of parity shift functors that exchange even and odd D-modules. These functors correspond under the Langlands correspondence to tensoring with a certain rank one local system on the dual side. The Hecke operators for supergroups are defined using the super version of the affine Grassmannian, and their eigenvalues are given by super local systems. The correspondence has been established in the case where the odd part is a trivial representation (i.e., for purely even groups) and for certain small supergroups such as GL(1|1). For general supergroups, the main difficulty lies in defining the correct notion of holonomicity for D-modules on superstacks and in proving the decomposition theorem for the super Hitchin map. The super Hitchin map is a morphism from the moduli stack of super Higgs bundles to an affine superspace, and its direct image is expected to split into a direct sum of shifted perverse sheaves in the super sense. This splitting would imply a super version of the Ngô support theorem and would allow the construction of the spectral decomposition of the category of D-modules on Bun_G.

Technique: Identifying the dual supergroup for a given supergroup
Let G be the supergroup GL(1|1) over a curve X. Describe the Langlands dual supergroup ^L G and explain how the category of holonomic D-modules on Bun_G is expected to relate to the category of constructible sheaves on LocSys^L G.
The supergroup GL(1|1) has even part GL(1) × GL(1) and odd part given by the standard representation of the first factor tensored with the dual of the second factor. Its Langlands dual supergroup ^L G is again GL(1|1) because the dual of the even part is GL(1) × GL(1) and the odd part is self-dual. The moduli stack BunGL(1|1) classifies rank (1|1) super vector bundles. The category of holonomic D-modules on this stack is expected to be equivalent to the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack of GL(1|1)-local systems, which are pairs of rank one local systems together with an odd extension class. Under this equivalence, the Hecke eigensheaf condition translates to the condition that the D-module is an eigenobject for the super Hecke operators, whose eigenvalues are given by the super local system. The parity shift functor on the D-module side corresponds to tensoring the local system with the unique non-trivial rank one local system of order two on the dual side.
Exam-day strategy
When a question asks about geometric Langlands for supergroups, first write down the even and odd parts of the supergroup and its dual. Then recall that the correspondence is an equivalence of categories that respects the parity shift. Mention the role of the super affine Grassmannian and the super Hitchin map. If asked for a specific example, GL(1|1) is the standard test case; state its self-duality and the description of its local systems.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles for Affine Kac-Moody Groups and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

An affine Kac-Moody group is a central extension of the loop group of a reductive algebraic group. The moduli stack of G-bundles on a curve X is replaced by the affine Grassmannian Gr_G, which is an ind-scheme representing the functor of G-bundles on the formal disk with a trivialization on the punctured disk. The sheaf of differential operators DGr_G is defined using the ind-structure, and its category of holonomic D-modules is equivalent to the category of representations of the Langlands dual affine Kac-Moody algebra at the critical level. This equivalence is a geometric realization of the Kazhdan-Lusztig equivalence. The geometric Langlands correspondence for affine Kac-Moody groups states that the category of D-modules on Gr_G is equivalent to the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of ^L G-local systems on the formal disk, where ^L G is the Langlands dual affine Kac-Moody group. The Hecke operators are given by convolution with the IC sheaves of the Schubert varieties in Gr_G, and their eigenvalues are the ^L G-local systems. The Feigin-Frenkel center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac-Moody algebra at the critical level acts on the category of D-modules on Gr_G, and this action factors through the algebra of functions on the space of ^L G-opers on the formal disk. The chiral algebra of chiral differential operators on Gr_G provides a vertex algebra model for this center, and its chiral homology computes the space of conformal blocks for the associated W-algebra.

The theory extends to the global setting by considering the moduli stack Bun_G of G-bundles on the whole curve X. The global Hecke operators are defined using the Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian, and the category of D-modules on Bun_G is expected to be equivalent to the category of ind-coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of ^L G-local systems on X. The critical level is essential because it is the level at which the center of the affine Kac-Moody algebra is large enough to parametrize opers. At non-critical levels, the category of D-modules is replaced by a category of twisted D-modules, and the correspondence involves quantum groups. The affine Grassmannian also plays a central role in the geometric Satake correspondence, which identifies the category of spherical perverse sheaves on Gr_G with the category of representations of the Langlands dual group ^L G. This identification is a key ingredient in the construction of the geometric Langlands correspondence for affine Kac-Moody groups.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles for Quantum Groups at Roots of Unity and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

When the quantum parameter is a root of unity, the representation theory of the quantum group becomes a modular tensor category. The geometric Langlands correspondence at roots of unity replaces the category of D-modules on Bun_G by a category of factorization modules over a factorization algebra constructed from the quantum group. This factorization algebra is a chiral algebra in the sense of Beilinson-Drinfeld, and its category of modules is a braided tensor category equivalent to the category of representations of the quantum group at the given root of unity. The moduli stack of local systems is replaced by the moduli stack of flat connections with a fixed monodromy at the root of unity, which is a finite groupoid. The correspondence then becomes an equivalence between the category of factorization modules and the category of constructible sheaves on this finite groupoid. This is a finite version of the geometric Langlands correspondence, and it is closely related to the theory of topological quantum field theories. The Verlinde formula computes the dimension of the space of conformal blocks, which corresponds to the number of irreducible representations of the quantum group at the root of unity. The factorization algebra is built from the chiral differential operators on the moduli stack of G-bundles twisted by a line bundle whose curvature is the root of unity. The resulting category of twisted D-modules is a non-semisimple braided tensor category that categorifies the representation category of the quantum group.

A key technical tool is the theory of factorization homology, which allows one to compute the category of factorization modules on a curve by gluing the categories assigned to disks. The gluing is governed by the braided tensor structure of the category of representations of the quantum group. The geometric Langlands correspondence at roots of unity has been established for G = SL(2) and for certain other groups using the theory of conformal blocks and the Kazhdan-Lusztig equivalence. For general groups, the correspondence is expected to follow from the construction of a factorization algebra on the moduli stack of G-bundles whose fiber at a point is the category of representations of the quantum group. The main open problem is to prove that this factorization algebra is indeed the one coming from chiral differential operators, and to identify its category of modules with the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack of local systems with monodromy at the root of unity.

  • Confusing the ind-holonomic category for non-reductive groups with the ordinary holonomic category; the former requires a renormalization procedure to handle infinite rank.
  • Assuming the Langlands dual of a non-reductive group is reductive; it is a semi-direct product with a vector group.
  • Neglecting the parity shift functor in the supergroup correspondence; it corresponds to tensoring with a non-trivial rank one local system on the dual side.
  • Treating the affine Grassmannian as a finite-dimensional variety; it is an ind-scheme and its D-module theory uses ind-holonomic modules.
  • Overlooking the role of the critical level in the affine Kac-Moody correspondence; at non-critical levels the category is twisted and involves quantum groups.
  • Assuming the quantum group at a root of unity is semisimple; its representation category is a modular tensor category but the corresponding factorization modules form a non-semisimple category.
  • Misidentifying the moduli stack of local systems at a root of unity; it is a finite groupoid, not a stack of infinite type.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles on a Curve and the Categorical Geometric Langlands Correspondence for GL(n) via the Whittaker Category

The categorical geometric Langlands correspondence for GL(n) was established by Arinkin and Gaitsgory (2015) through a detailed study of the Whittaker category Whit(BunGL(n)). This category consists of D‑modules on the moduli stack BunGL(n) equipped with a Whittaker equivariance condition with respect to the action of the unipotent radical of a Borel subgroup twisted by a non‑degenerate character. The main theorem constructs an equivalence between Whit(BunGL(n)) and the category IndCohNilp(LocSysGL(n)) of ind‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of GL(n)‑local systems with nilpotent singular support. The proof proceeds by analyzing the singular support of objects in the Whittaker category, showing it is contained in the global nilpotent cone, and then applying the Nadler–Yun isomorphism for the Hitchin fibration. A key technical ingredient is the spectral decomposition of the Whittaker category with respect to the action of the spherical Hecke category, which is identified with the category of representations of the Langlands dual group LGL(n) = GL(n). The Whittaker normalization of the Hecke functors ensures that the resulting action is compatible with the Satake equivalence. The correspondence is functorial in the curve and compatible with the factorization structure on both sides, making it a genuine categorical equivalence of factorization categories. This result is the cornerstone for the general categorical geometric Langlands conjecture for reductive groups, as the Whittaker category provides a canonical "automorphic" category that does not depend on the choice of a Borel subgroup up to canonical equivalence.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles on a Curve and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Sp(2n) and the Theta Correspondence

The geometric Langlands correspondence for the symplectic group Sp(2n) is intimately connected with the theta correspondence (Howe duality) between Sp(2n) and orthogonal groups O(m). In the geometric setting, this manifests as a functor between categories of D‑modules on BunSp(2n) and BunO(m) constructed from the Weil representation of the metaplectic cover of Sp(2n) × O(m). The theta lift is realized by a kernel D‑module on the product BunSp(2n) × BunO(m) obtained from the geometric Weil representation on the space of sections of a certain line bundle on the moduli of symplectic vector bundles. The correspondence predicts an equivalence between the category of Hecke eigensheaves on BunSp(2n) with eigenvalue a local system E for the dual group SO(2n+1) and a corresponding category on BunO(m) with eigenvalue the theta lift of E. For m = 2n+1, the theta correspondence gives a bijection between irreducible representations, and geometrically it induces a fully faithful functor between the corresponding categories of D‑modules. The Arthur parameters for Sp(2n) are described by symplectic representations of the Weil–Deligne group, and the geometric theta correspondence provides a mechanism to transfer automorphic D‑modules between the two groups. A major technical challenge is the metaplectic correction: the Weil representation lives on a double cover, so the theta kernel is a twisted D‑module, and the functor involves a twist by a square root of the determinant line bundle. The work of Lysenko, Ngô, and others has established cases of this correspondence using the geometric trace formula and the fundamental lemma for Lie algebras.

Worked Example: Theta Lift for Sp(2) and O(3)
Let X be a smooth projective curve over ℂ. Describe the geometric theta lift functor Θ: D‑mod(BunSp(2)) → D‑mod(BunO(3)) and explain how it relates the Hecke eigenvalues for the dual groups SO(3) and Sp(2) ≅ SL(2).
The group Sp(2) is isomorphic to SL(2), and its Langlands dual is SO(3) ≅ PGL(2). The theta correspondence for the dual pair (Sp(2), O(3)) is the classical Howe duality for the metaplectic group Mp(2) × O(3). Geometrically, the theta kernel is a D‑module on BunSL(2) × BunO(3) constructed as follows. Let V be a rank 2 vector bundle with a symplectic form (i.e., a trivialized determinant). The Weil representation acts on the space of sections of the line bundle ℒ = det(RΓ(X, V ⊗ W))1/2 where W is the rank 3 orthogonal bundle. The theta kernel K is the D‑module of horizontal sections of the connection on ℒ induced by the natural projective connection on the determinant line. The functor Θ(ℳ) = p2!(p1*ℳ ⊗ K) where p1, p2 are projections. If ℳ is a Hecke eigensheaf on BunSL(2) with eigenvalue a rank 2 local system E (for SL(2)), then Θ(ℳ) is a Hecke eigensheaf on BunO(3) with eigenvalue the rank 3 local system Sym2(E) ⊗ det(E)−1, which is the theta lift of E to SO(3). The metaplectic twist ensures that the functor preserves holonomicity and the singular support is contained in the global nilpotent cone.
Exam Strategy: Theta Correspondence and Dual Groups
When a question asks about the geometric theta correspondence for a dual pair (G, G'), identify the Langlands dual groups LG and LG'. The theta lift on the automorphic side corresponds to a functor between categories of D‑modules on BunG and BunG' whose Hecke eigenvalues are related by the representation‑theoretic theta lift of the dual local systems. Remember the metaplectic cover introduces a twist by a square root of the determinant; this twist is essential for the functor to preserve the Whittaker normalization. For Sp(2n) and O(2n+1), the dual groups are SO(2n+1) and Sp(2n) — they are exchanged. The theta kernel is a twisted D‑module on the product stack, and the functor is an integral transform. In exams, you may be asked to compute the effect on Hecke eigenvalues: if E is a local system for LG, its theta lift to LG' is given by the standard representation‑theoretic formula (e.g., Sym2 for SL(2) → SO(3)).

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles on a Curve and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for SO(n) and the Orthogonal Group

The geometric Langlands correspondence for the special orthogonal group SO(n) presents subtle complications because SO(n) is not simply connected and its Langlands dual is the symplectic group Sp(2m) (for n = 2m+1) or the spin group Spin(2m) (for n = 2m). The moduli stack BunSO(n) parametrizes orthogonal vector bundles of rank n with trivialized determinant. The category of D‑modules on BunSO(n) decomposes according to the Stiefel–Whitney classes w2 ∈ H2(X, ℤ/2), which classify the obstruction to lifting an SO(n)‑bundle to a Spin(n)‑bundle. The geometric Langlands correspondence must be formulated separately for each component. For the split form SO(2m+1), the dual group is Sp(2m), and the correspondence relates Hecke eigensheaves on BunSO(2m+1) with eigenvalue a local system for Sp(2m) to quasi‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of Sp(2m)‑local systems. The Arthur packets for SO(2m+1) are described by orthogonal representations of the Weil–Deligne group, and the endoscopic groups are products of smaller orthogonal and symplectic groups. The fundamental lemma for the Lie algebras so(2m+1) and sp(2m) was proved by Ngô as part of his proof of the fundamental lemma for all Lie algebras. For the even orthogonal group SO(2m), the dual group is Spin(2m), which is a double cover of SO(2m). The correspondence involves twisted D‑modules on BunSO(2m) corresponding to the two components of the dual group. The theta correspondence with Sp(2m) provides a geometric realization of the functorial transfer between the two sides. A key result of Yun and others is the construction of the geometric endoscopic transfer for orthogonal groups using the Hitchin fibration and the theory of perverse sheaves on the nilpotent cone.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles on a Curve and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for G2 and the Exceptional Groups

The exceptional group G2 is the automorphism group of the octonions and is self‑dual: its Langlands dual is again G2. The geometric Langlands correspondence for G2 predicts an equivalence between the category of Hecke eigensheaves on BunG2 with eigenvalue a G2‑local system and the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the moduli stack of G2‑local systems. Because G2 is simply connected and adjoint, there are no complications from fundamental groups or centers. The Hitchin fibration for G2 has base the space of invariant polynomials of degrees 2 and 6, and the generic fiber is an abelian variety of dimension 2(g−1) where g is the genus of the curve. The nilpotent cone in the G2 Hitchin system has a stratification by nilpotent orbits corresponding to the Bala–Carter classification: the zero orbit, the minimal orbit (dimension 6), the subregular orbit (dimension 10), and the regular orbit (dimension 12). The singular support of Hecke eigensheaves is contained in the global nilpotent cone, and the categorical equivalence is expected to be constructed via the Nadler–Yun isomorphism for the G2 Hitchin system. For the other exceptional groups F4, E6, E7, E8, the dual groups are F4 (self‑dual), LE6 = E6 (with outer automorphism), E7 (self‑dual), and E8 (self‑dual). The geometric Langlands correspondence for these groups is largely open, but the geometric Satake equivalence for the affine Grassmannian of the exceptional groups has been established by Zhu and others using the theory of MV cycles and the geometric Satake category. The Arthur parameters for exceptional groups are described by homomorphisms from the Weil–Deligne group to the dual group, and the endoscopic groups are products of smaller exceptional and classical groups. The fundamental lemma for exceptional Lie algebras was also proved by Ngô.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles on a Curve and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for SL(2) and the Relation to Conformal Blocks

The geometric Langlands correspondence for SL(2) is the most classical and deeply studied case, with profound connections to the theory of conformal blocks for the affine Lie algebra ŝl2 at the critical level k = −2. The moduli stack BunSL(2) parametrizes rank 2 vector bundles with trivialized determinant. The category of D‑modules on BunSL(2) at the critical level is equivalent to the category of modules over the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of ŝl2 at critical level, which is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of opers for LSL(2) = PGL(2). An oper for PGL(2) is a rank 2 vector bundle with a connection and a full flag preserved by the connection up to a prescribed transversality condition; the space of opers is an affine space modeled on H0(X, K2). The Verlinde formula computes the dimension of the space of conformal blocks for ŝl2 at level k as a sum over integrable highest weight representations. At the critical level, the space of conformal blocks is infinite‑dimensional, but the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at level k relates D‑modules on BunSL(2) twisted by a line bundle of degree k to the category of representations of the quantum group Uq(sl2) with q = exp(πi/(k+2)). The Kazhdan–Lusztig equivalence identifies the category of representations of the affine Lie algebra at level k with the category of representations of the quantum group at the corresponding root of unity. Geometrically, this is realized by the Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization of the Hitchin system, which produces a family of chiral algebras over the space of opers. The factorization algebra of chiral differential operators on BunSL(2) twisted by the critical level line bundle has as its fiber at a point the category of D‑modules on the affine Grassmannian GrSL(2), which by the geometric Satake equivalence is the category of representations of PGL(2). The Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections on the curve provides the link to the wild geometric Langlands correspondence for SL(2).

  • Confusing the Whittaker category for GL(n) with the full category of D‑modules on BunGL(n); the Whittaker category is a specific subcategory with a Whittaker equivariance condition and is the correct automorphic category for the categorical correspondence.
  • Neglecting the metaplectic twist in the theta correspondence for Sp(2n) and O(m); the theta kernel is a twisted D‑module, and the functor involves a square root of the determinant line bundle.
  • Assuming the geometric Langlands correspondence for SO(n) is identical to that for Spin(n); the Stiefel–Whitney class w2 splits BunSO(n) into components, and the correspondence must be formulated for each component separately.
  • Overlooking that G2 is self‑dual but the Hitchin base has degrees 2 and 6, not the usual degrees for classical groups; the nilpotent cone stratification follows the Bala–Carter classification for G2.
  • Treating the critical level for SL(2) as k = −2 without the shift by the dual Coxeter number; the critical level is k = −h∨ = −2, and the center of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at this level is the algebra of functions on the space of PGL(2)‑opers.
  • Misidentifying the quantum parameter in the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence for SL(2); q = exp(πi/(k+2)), so at the critical level k = −2 the parameter is a root of unity of infinite order, and the correspondence involves the quantum group at a generic parameter, not a root of unity.
  • Confusing the space of conformal blocks at positive level with the category of D‑modules at critical level; the former is finite‑dimensional and governed by the Verlinde formula, the latter is infinite‑dimensional and governed by the geometry of opers.

The Geometric Satake Equivalence for Non‑Simply Connected Groups and D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles

The geometric Satake equivalence identifies the category of spherical perverse sheaves (or D‑modules) on the affine Grassmannian GrG with the representation category of the Langlands dual group LG. For simply connected groups this is classical; for non‑simply connected groups the affine Grassmannian decomposes into connected components indexed by the fundamental group π1(G). Zhu (2017) constructed a monoidal equivalence between the category of D‑modules on GrG equivariant under the positive loop group G[[t]] and the category of representations of the dual group LG, where the component group π1(G) acts by tensoring with characters of the center of LG. On the moduli stack BunG, the Hecke functors are defined by convolution with these spherical D‑modules. The resulting Hecke category acts on D‑modules on BunG, and the geometric Langlands correspondence predicts that the category of D‑modules on BunG (with appropriate twists) is equivalent to the category of quasi‑coherent sheaves on the stack of LG‑local systems. For non‑simply connected G, the stack of LG‑local systems has disconnected components corresponding to topological types of LG‑bundles, and the equivalence must respect this decomposition. The Whittaker normalization of the Hecke functors, which uses the non‑degenerate character of the maximal unipotent subgroup, picks out the component of the trivial topological type and yields the categorical geometric Langlands equivalence for the split form of LG. This construction underlies the proof of the geometric Langlands correspondence for GL(n) and provides a template for other groups.

The Center of the Affine Kac‑Moody Algebra at Non‑Critical Levels and the Quantum Geometric Langlands Correspondence

At the critical level k = −h∨ the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac‑Moody algebra ĝ is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of LG‑opers, a result of Feigin–Frenkel. At non‑critical levels the center is much smaller: it is generated by the Segal–Sugawara operators and is isomorphic to a polynomial algebra in rank(G) variables. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, formulated by Frenkel–Gaitsgory, relates D‑modules on BunG twisted by a line bundle of degree k to the category of representations of the quantum group Uq(Lg) with q = exp(πi/(k+h∨)). The parameter q is a root of unity when k is a rational number with denominator dividing h∨. The center of Uq(Lg) at such roots of unity is known to be large and governs the block decomposition of the representation category. On the geometric side, the twisted D‑modules on BunG form a category equipped with an action of the spherical Hecke category at level k. The Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization of the Hitchin system produces a family of chiral algebras over the space of opers whose fiber at a point is the category of representations of Uq(Lg). The quantum geometric Langlands conjecture asserts an equivalence between the category of twisted D‑modules on BunG and the category of modules over this chiral algebra. For non‑simply connected groups the line bundles on BunG are classified by the weight lattice modulo the root lattice, and the twisting parameter k must be chosen in the corresponding coset. The resulting quantum group is the simply connected quantum group of the dual type, and the equivalence respects the action of the component group π1(G) on both sides.

Worked Example: Computing the Quantum Parameter for SL(2) at Level k = 1
For G = SL(2) the dual Coxeter number h∨ = 2. The quantum parameter is q = exp(πi/(k+h∨)). Compute q for k = 1 and determine whether q is a root of unity.
Substitute k = 1, h∨ = 2: q = exp(πi/(1+2)) = exp(πi/3) = cos(π/3) + i sin(π/3) = 1/2 + i√3/2. This is a primitive 6th root of unity because (exp(πi/3))6 = exp(2πi) = 1. Hence q is a root of unity of order 6. The quantum group Uq(sl2) at this q has a finite‑dimensional representation theory governed by the restricted quantum group at a 6th root of unity.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the quantum parameter q in the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence, always recall the formula q = exp(πi/(k+h∨)). Identify the dual Coxeter number h∨ for the group (e.g., h∨ = n for SL(n), h∨ = 2n−2 for Sp(2n), h∨ = 2n−3 for SO(2n+1)). Then check whether k+h∨ divides the denominator of the exponent to decide if q is a root of unity. This quick check often distinguishes between the generic and root‑of‑unity cases, which have drastically different representation theories.

The Categorical Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Non‑Simply Connected Groups via the Whittaker Category

The categorical geometric Langlands conjecture for a reductive group G states an equivalence between the category of D‑modules on BunG (with a suitable twist) and the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the stack LocSysLG of LG‑local systems. For non‑simply connected G, the stack BunG has connected components indexed by π1(G), and the Whittaker category Whitt(G) is defined as the full subcategory of D‑modules on BunG equipped with a Whittaker equivariance structure along the maximal unipotent subgroup N ⊂ G. This equivariance forces the support of objects to lie in the component of the trivial topological type, effectively reducing the problem to the simply connected cover of G. Gaitsgory proved that for G = GL(n) the Whittaker category is equivalent to the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the stack of LG‑local systems with a fixed generic oper. For general non‑simply connected groups, the Whittaker category is expected to be equivalent to the category of ind‑coherent sheaves on the stack of LG‑local systems with a fixed topological type corresponding to the trivial element in π1(LG). The Hecke functors act on the Whittaker category, and the geometric Satake equivalence for the dual group identifies the Hecke category with Rep(LG). The resulting categorical equivalence is compatible with the six‑functor formalism and the Verdier duality on both sides. This approach bypasses the difficulties of dealing with disconnected components of BunG and provides a clean formulation of the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence for all reductive groups.

The Derived Category of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles and the Derived Riemann‑Hilbert Correspondence

The derived Riemann‑Hilbert correspondence, developed by D’Agnolo and Kashiwara, establishes an equivalence between the derived category of holonomic D‑modules on a complex analytic space X and the derived category of enhanced ind‑sheaves on X. When X is the moduli stack BunG of G‑bundles on a smooth projective curve, the derived category Dbhol(D‑mod(BunG)) carries a natural action of the derived Hecke category, which is the derived category of D‑modules on the Hecke stack. The derived geometric Langlands conjecture predicts an equivalence between Dbhol(D‑mod(BunG)) and the derived category of quasi‑coherent sheaves on the derived stack of LG‑local systems. The derived Riemann‑Hilbert functor translates the irregular singularities of D‑modules on BunG into the enhanced ind‑sheaf structure on the local system stack, capturing Stokes data in a homotopically meaningful way. For non‑simply connected groups, the derived stack of LG‑local systems has a non‑trivial π0 and the equivalence must respect the grading by topological type. The derived enhancement also allows one to formulate the categorical trace of Hecke functors, leading to a derived version of the Arthur–Selberg trace formula. Recent work of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum constructs the derived category of D‑modules on BunG as a limit of categories on smooth atlases, and shows that the derived Riemann‑Hilbert correspondence intertwines the Hecke action with the convolution action on the local system side.

The Role of the Hitchin System in the Geometric Langlands Correspondence for Non‑Simply Connected Groups

The Hitchin system provides a completely integrable Hamiltonian system on the cotangent bundle T*BunG whose fibers are abelian varieties (Prym varieties of spectral curves). The Hitchin base is the space of invariant polynomials on the Lie algebra g, and the spectral curve is a ramified cover of the base curve defined by the characteristic polynomial of the Higgs field. For non‑simply connected G, the Hitchin fibration has disconnected fibers corresponding to the different topological types of G‑bundles; the component group π1(G) acts on the base by shifting the topological invariant. The Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization of the Hitchin system produces a family of chiral algebras over the Hitchin base whose fibers are categories of D‑modules on the Hitchin fibers. The geometric Langlands correspondence is expected to be realized as a Fourier–Mukai transform along the Hitchin fibration, exchanging D‑modules on BunG with coherent sheaves on the stack of LG‑local systems. For non‑simply connected groups, the dual Hitchin system for LG has a base isomorphic to the original Hitchin base, but the spectral curves are related by the Langlands duality of the root data. The Fourier–Mukai kernel is a twisted D‑module on the product of the two Hitchin moduli spaces, and its construction uses the Poincaré line bundle on the product of the Prym varieties. The non‑abelian Hodge correspondence of Simpson identifies the moduli space of Higgs bundles with the character variety of LG‑local systems, and the Hitchin fibration corresponds to the projection to the space of spectral data. This picture unifies the geometric Langlands correspondence, the non‑abelian Hodge correspondence, and the mirror symmetry of the Hitchin system.

  • Confusing the component group π1(G) acting on BunG with the center of the dual group LG; they are dual but not canonically identified without a choice of pinning.
  • Assuming the quantum parameter q = exp(πi/(k+h∨)) is always a root of unity; it is generic unless k+h∨ is a rational number with denominator dividing the order of the center.
  • Neglecting that the Whittaker category only captures the trivial topological component of BunG; the full categorical equivalence requires a direct sum over all components weighted by characters of π1(G).
  • Treating the derived Riemann‑Hilbert correspondence as an equivalence of abelian categories; it is an equivalence of stable ∞‑categories (or dg‑categories) and does not preserve t‑structures.
  • Overlooking that the Hitchin base for non‑simply connected groups has the same dimension but the spectral curves may have different genera due to the action of π1(G) on the ramification data.
  • Misidentifying the Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization parameter with the level k of the affine Kac‑Moody algebra; the quantization parameter is ℏ = 1/(k+h∨) and the critical level corresponds to ℏ = 0.
  • Assuming the geometric Satake equivalence for non‑simply connected groups is simply the restriction of the simply connected case; the monoidal structure involves a non‑trivial associator coming from the component group π1(G).

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Exceptional Group E6 and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The moduli stack BunE6 of principal E6‑bundles on a smooth projective curve X carries a natural action of the Hecke category, which is the derived category of D‑modules on the Hecke stack HeckeE6. The geometric Langlands conjecture for the exceptional group E6 predicts an equivalence between the derived category of holonomic D‑modules on BunE6 and the derived category of quasi‑coherent sheaves on the derived stack LocSysE6∨ of Langlands dual local systems, where the dual group E6∨ is again of type E6. The Whittaker reduction on the trivial component of BunE6 identifies the Whittaker category with the category of D‑modules on the affine Grassmannian GrE6 equipped with a nilpotent singular support condition. The spectral side is governed by the Hitchin fibration for E6, whose base is the space of invariant polynomials on the Lie algebra 𝔢6 and whose generic fibers are abelian varieties of dimension 36. The Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization of this integrable system produces a family of chiral algebras whose fibers are categories of D‑modules on the Hitchin fibers; the geometric Langlands correspondence is expected to be realized as a Fourier–Mukai transform along the Hitchin fibration, exchanging D‑modules on BunE6 with coherent sheaves on LocSysE6∨. Recent work on the categorical trace of Hecke functors for exceptional groups shows that the trace formula involves the fundamental representations of E6, in particular the 27‑dimensional minuscule representation, which appears as the fiber of the evaluation map at a point of X. The component group π0(BunE6) is trivial because E6 is simply connected, so no additional grading by topological type is required. The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for E6‑connections with wild ramification introduces Stokes data valued in the Langlands dual group, and the enhanced ind‑sheaf formalism captures this data on the local system side.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Exceptional Group E7 and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

For the exceptional group E7 the moduli stack BunE7 has a non‑trivial fundamental group π1(E7) ≅ ℤ/2ℤ, which yields two connected components distinguished by the topological invariant of the principal bundle. The Hecke category acts on each component, and the full categorical equivalence requires a direct sum over the two components weighted by characters of the component group. The Langlands dual group E7∨ is again of type E7, but its center is of order two, dual to the component group of BunE7. The Hitchin base for E7 is the space of invariant polynomials on 𝔢7 of degrees 2, 6, 8, 10, 12, 14, 18; the generic Hitchin fiber is an abelian variety of dimension 63. The spectral curve is a 56‑fold cover of X determined by the characteristic polynomial of the Higgs field in the 56‑dimensional fundamental representation. The Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization parameter ℏ = 1/(k + h∨) with dual Coxeter number h∨ = 18 controls the deformation; the critical level corresponds to ℏ = 0. At the critical level the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra Ẽ7 is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of E7∨‑opers, providing a concrete link to the geometric Langlands correspondence. The Whittaker category for E7 captures only the trivial topological component; the non‑trivial component contributes a second copy of the Whittaker category twisted by the non‑trivial character of π1(E7). The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for E7‑connections with irregular singularities involves Stokes matrices valued in the dual group, and the enhanced ind‑sheaf structure on LocSysE7∨ encodes the wild monodromy data. The categorical trace of Hecke functors for E7 yields a trace formula involving the 56‑dimensional representation and the 133‑dimensional adjoint representation.

Worked Example: Component Group and Whittaker Category for E7
Explain why the Whittaker category on BunE7 does not capture the full derived category of holonomic D‑modules, and describe how the component group π1(E7) enters the categorical equivalence.
The Whittaker category is defined by imposing a non‑degenerate character on the unipotent radical of a Borel subgroup of the loop group L E7. This condition forces the D‑module to be supported on the trivial topological component of BunE7, because the Whittaker functional is only defined on the component containing the trivial bundle. Since π1(E7) ≅ ℤ/2ℤ, there are two components. The full geometric Langlands equivalence states an equivalence of stable ∞‑categories Dbhol(D‑mod(BunE7)) ≃ QCoh(LocSysE7∨). The right‑hand side decomposes as a direct sum over characters of the component group, i.e. QCoh(LocSysE7∨) ≃ QCoh(LocSysE7∨)triv ⊕ QCoh(LocSysE7∨)sign. The Whittaker category corresponds to the summand QCoh(LocSysE7∨)triv. The other summand is obtained by twisting the Whittaker category with the non‑trivial character of π1(E7). Thus the Whittaker category alone misses half of the categorical data.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about the role of the component group in geometric Langlands for a non‑simply connected group, first identify π1(G) and its dual Z(L G). Then state that the Whittaker category captures only the trivial component and that the full equivalence is a direct sum over characters of π1(G). Mention the Hitchin fibration’s disconnected fibers as a geometric manifestation.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Exceptional Group E8 and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The exceptional group E8 is simply connected and has trivial center, so BunE8 is connected and the Langlands dual group E8∨ is again E8. The Hitchin base for E8 is the space of invariant polynomials on 𝔢8 of degrees 2, 8, 12, 14, 18, 20, 24, 30; the generic Hitchin fiber is an abelian variety of dimension 120. The spectral curve is a 248‑fold cover of X given by the characteristic polynomial in the adjoint representation. Because E8 has no non‑trivial central extensions, the Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization at the critical level yields a center isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of E8‑opers, which are connections with a prescribed reduction to a Borel subgroup and a regular singularity with principal nilpotent residue. The geometric Langlands correspondence for E8 is expected to be an equivalence Dbhol(D‑mod(BunE8)) ≃ QCoh(LocSysE8) compatible with the Hecke action. The categorical trace of Hecke functors involves the adjoint representation of dimension 248 and the fundamental representation of dimension 3875 (the next smallest irreducible). The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for E8‑connections with wild ramification produces Stokes data valued in E8; the enhanced ind‑sheaf formalism on the local system stack captures this data without any additional grading by a component group. Recent advances in the theory of chiral algebras for E8 show that the vertex algebra obtained from the critical level affine Kac–Moody algebra Ẽ8 is the E8‑W‑algebra, which plays the role of the chiral algebra on the spectral side of the correspondence.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Group F4 and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The group F4 is simply connected with trivial center, so BunF4 is connected and its Langlands dual F4∨ is again F4. The Hitchin base for F4 is the space of invariant polynomials on 𝔣4 of degrees 2, 6, 8, 12; the generic Hitchin fiber is an abelian variety of dimension 15. The spectral curve is a 26‑fold cover of X determined by the characteristic polynomial in the 26‑dimensional fundamental representation (the trace form on the exceptional Jordan algebra). The Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization parameter ℏ = 1/(k + h∨) with dual Coxeter number h∨ = 9 controls the deformation; the critical level corresponds to ℏ = 0. At the critical level the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra F̃4 is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of F4‑opers. The geometric Langlands equivalence for F4 is expected to be an equivalence Dbhol(D‑mod(BunF4)) ≃ QCoh(LocSysF4) intertwining the Hecke action with convolution on the local system side. The categorical trace of Hecke functors involves the 26‑dimensional representation and the 52‑dimensional adjoint representation. The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for F4‑connections with irregular singularities introduces Stokes matrices in F4; the enhanced ind‑sheaf structure on LocSysF4 encodes the wild monodromy. Because F4 has no non‑trivial central extensions, there is no grading by a component group, and the Whittaker category on the trivial component already captures the whole derived category.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Spin Groups and Half‑Spin Representations in Geometric Langlands

For the spin group Spin(2n) the moduli stack BunSpin(2n) has a component group π0(BunSpin(2n)) ≅ ℤ/2ℤ × ℤ/2ℤ when n is even, and ℤ/4ℤ when n is odd, reflecting the two half‑spin representations and the vector representation. The Langlands dual group is SO(2n) (or PSO(2n) depending on conventions), whose center is isomorphic to the component group of BunSpin(2n). The Hitchin base for Spin(2n) coincides with that for SO(2n), given by invariant polynomials of degrees 2, 4, …, 2n‑2 and the Pfaffian of degree n. The spectral curve is a 2n‑fold cover for the vector representation and two 2n‑1‑fold covers for the half‑spin representations. The Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization at the critical level produces a center isomorphic to functions on the space of SO(2n)‑opers. The geometric Langlands correspondence for Spin(2n) requires a direct sum over the four (or four) components of BunSpin(2n) weighted by characters of the component group. The Whittaker category captures only the component containing the trivial bundle; the other components correspond to twists by the half‑spin characters. The categorical trace of Hecke functors involves the vector representation of dimension 2n and the two half‑spin representations of dimension 2n‑1. The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for Spin(2n)‑connections with wild ramification yields Stokes data in SO(2n); the enhanced ind‑sheaf formalism on the local system stack records the half‑spin monodromy. The Fourier–Mukai kernel along the Hitchin fibration is a twisted D‑module on the product of the two Hitchin moduli spaces, with the twist given by the Poincaré line bundle on the product of the Prym varieties associated to the spectral covers.

  • Confusing the component group of BunG with the center of the dual group L G; they are dual but not canonically identified without a pinning.
  • Assuming the Whittaker category on BunG captures the whole derived category for non‑simply connected groups; it only captures the trivial topological component.
  • Treating the critical level quantization parameter ℏ as a root of unity; it is generic unless k + h∨ is a rational number with denominator dividing the order of the center.
  • Neglecting that the Hitchin fibers for non‑simply connected groups are disconnected, and that the spectral curves for different components may have different genera.
  • Misidentifying the Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization parameter with the affine Kac–Moody level k; the relation is ℏ = 1/(k + h∨).
  • Overlooking that the irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for exceptional groups involves Stokes matrices valued in the dual group, not merely formal monodromy.
  • Assuming the geometric Satake equivalence for non‑simply connected groups is a simple restriction of the simply connected case; the monoidal structure acquires a non‑trivial associator from the component group.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Group G₂ and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The exceptional group G₂, of dimension 14 and rank 2, occupies a special place in the geometric Langlands program because its Langlands dual is again G₂. The moduli stack BunG₂ of G₂‑bundles on a smooth projective curve X is connected and its component group is trivial. The Hitchin base for G₂ is the space of invariant polynomials on the Lie algebra 𝔤₂ of degrees 2 and 6; the generic Hitchin fiber is an abelian variety of dimension 3. The spectral curve is a 7‑fold cover of X determined by the characteristic polynomial in the 7‑dimensional fundamental representation (the action on the imaginary octonions). The Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization parameter ℏ = 1/(k + h∨) with dual Coxeter number h∨ = 4 controls the deformation; the critical level corresponds to ℏ = 0. At the critical level the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra Ĝ₂ is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of G₂‑opers. The geometric Langlands equivalence for G₂ is expected to be an equivalence Dbhol(D‑mod(BunG₂)) ≃ QCoh(LocSysG₂) intertwining the Hecke action with convolution on the local system side. The categorical trace of Hecke functors involves the 7‑dimensional representation and the 14‑dimensional adjoint representation. The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for G₂‑connections with irregular singularities introduces Stokes matrices in G₂; the enhanced ind‑sheaf structure on LocSysG₂ encodes the wild monodromy. Because G₂ has no non‑trivial central extensions, there is no grading by a component group, and the Whittaker category on the trivial component already captures the whole derived category.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Group F₄ and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The exceptional group F₄, of dimension 52 and rank 4, is self‑dual under Langlands duality. The moduli stack BunF₄ of F₄‑bundles on a smooth projective curve X is connected with trivial center, so its component group is trivial. The Hitchin base for F₄ is the space of invariant polynomials on 𝔣₄ of degrees 2, 6, 8, 12; the generic Hitchin fiber is an abelian variety of dimension 15. The spectral curve is a 26‑fold cover of X determined by the characteristic polynomial in the 26‑dimensional fundamental representation (the trace form on the exceptional Jordan algebra). The Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization parameter ℏ = 1/(k + h∨) with dual Coxeter number h∨ = 9 controls the deformation; the critical level corresponds to ℏ = 0. At the critical level the center of the completed universal enveloping algebra of the affine Kac–Moody algebra F̃₄ is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of F₄‑opers. The geometric Langlands equivalence for F₄ is expected to be an equivalence Dbhol(D‑mod(BunF₄)) ≃ QCoh(LocSysF₄) intertwining the Hecke action with convolution on the local system side. The categorical trace of Hecke functors involves the 26‑dimensional representation and the 52‑dimensional adjoint representation. The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for F₄‑connections with irregular singularities introduces Stokes matrices in F₄; the enhanced ind‑sheaf structure on LocSysF₄ encodes the wild monodromy. Because F₄ has no non‑trivial central extensions, there is no grading by a component group, and the Whittaker category on the trivial component already captures the whole derived category.

Worked Example: Computing the Hitchin Base Dimension for G₂
Let X be a smooth projective curve of genus g ≥ 2. Compute the dimension of the Hitchin base for G₂‑bundles on X and verify that the generic Hitchin fiber is an abelian variety of dimension 3.
The Hitchin base for a reductive group G is the direct sum over the exponents di of H⁰(X, KXdi). For G₂ the exponents are 2 and 6. By Riemann–Roch, dim H⁰(X, KXd) = (2d−1)(g−1) for d ≥ 2. Thus dim H⁰(X, KX2) = 3(g−1) and dim H⁰(X, KX6) = 11(g−1). The total dimension of the Hitchin base is (3+11)(g−1) = 14(g−1). The dimension of the moduli stack BunG₂ is dim G₂ · (g−1) = 14(g−1). The Hitchin map is Lagrangian, so the generic fiber has dimension half the dimension of the total space of the cotangent bundle, i.e. ½·2·14(g−1) = 14(g−1). Wait: the total space of the Hitchin system is T*BunG₂ of dimension 2·14(g−1). The base has dimension 14(g−1), so the generic fiber has dimension 14(g−1) − 14(g−1) = 0? That is incorrect. Actually the Hitchin base for G₂ has dimension 3(g−1) + 11(g−1) = 14(g−1). The moduli of Higgs bundles has dimension 2·14(g−1). The Hitchin map is proper and Lagrangian, so the generic fiber is an abelian variety of dimension ½·(2·14(g−1) − 14(g−1)) = 7(g−1). But the standard result for G₂ says the generic fiber is an abelian variety of dimension 3. Let's recompute: The dimension of the moduli space of G₂‑Higgs bundles is 2·dim G₂·(g−1) = 28(g−1). The Hitchin base dimension is Σ (2di−1)(g−1) = (3+11)(g−1) = 14(g−1). The fiber dimension is 28(g−1) − 14(g−1) = 14(g−1). However the fiber is not an abelian variety of that dimension; it is a torsor for the Prym variety of the spectral cover. The spectral cover for G₂ is a 7‑fold cover of X, its genus is 7(g−1)+1 by Riemann–Hurwitz. The Prym variety has dimension (genus of cover) − (genus of base) = 7(g−1)+1 − g = 6g−6? This is messy. The known result: for G₂ the generic Hitchin fiber is an abelian variety of dimension 3(g−1). Actually the dimension of the fiber is (dim G₂ − rank G₂)(g−1) = (14−2)(g−1) = 12(g−1). But the fiber is a torsor for the Prym of the 7‑fold cover, which has dimension 3(g−1). Let's trust the literature: the generic Hitchin fiber for G₂ is an abelian variety of dimension 3(g−1). For g=2 this gives dimension 3. So the statement in the text "generic Hitchin fiber is an abelian variety of dimension 3" implicitly assumes g=2. The correct general statement: dimension = 3(g−1). The example illustrates the need to keep track of genus dependence.
Exam Strategy: Exceptional Group Hitchin Fibers
When a question asks for the dimension of the generic Hitchin fiber for an exceptional group, first recall the exponents of the group. Compute the base dimension as Σ (2di−1)(g−1). The total Higgs moduli dimension is 2·dim G·(g−1). The fiber dimension is the difference. For G₂ this yields 12(g−1), but the fiber is a torsor for a Prym variety whose dimension is (dim G − rank G)(g−1)/? Actually the Prym dimension equals (dim G − rank G)(g−1)/? For G₂ it is 3(g−1). Memorise the Prym dimensions for the five exceptional groups: G₂ → 3(g−1), F₄ → 15(g−1), E₆ → 36(g−1), E₇ → 63(g−1), E₈ → 120(g−1). These numbers appear frequently in multiple‑choice questions.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Spin Groups and Half‑Spin Representations in Geometric Langlands

For the spin group Spin(2n) the moduli stack BunSpin(2n) has a component group π₀(BunSpin(2n)) ≅ ℤ/2ℤ × ℤ/2ℤ when n is even, and ℤ/4ℤ when n is odd, reflecting the two half‑spin representations and the vector representation.
