The Fock–Goncharov conjecture, proved in many cases, states that the cluster Poisson variety LocSysG has a canonical basis of regular functions parametrised by the tropical points of the dual cluster variety, which are the integral laminations on Σ. Under the geometric Langlands correspondence, this basis corresponds to the basis of the Grothendieck group of D‑mod(BunG) given by the classes of the standard D‑modules associated to the strata of the Harder–Narasimhan stratification. The quantum dilogarithm also appears in the wall‑crossing formula for the Donaldson–Thomas invariants of the 3d Calabi–Yau category associated to the quiver with potential of the triangulation, linking the cluster structure to the Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Geometric Langlands Correspondence with Wall‑Crossing Formulas and the Kontsevich–Soibelman Wall‑Crossing

The Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing formula describes how the Donaldson–Thomas invariants of a 3d Calabi–Yau category change as the stability condition varies across a wall of marginal stability. In the context of the geometric Langlands correspondence, the relevant 3d Calabi–Yau category is the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack BunG with a fixed central character, or equivalently the category of coherent sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysG with a fixed monodromy at the punctures. The stability condition is given by the choice of a point in the Hitchin base, i.e. a choice of spectral curve. The walls of marginal stability correspond to the loci in the Hitchin base where the spectral curve becomes singular or where the abelian variety fibre of the Hitchin fibration acquires extra automorphisms. The Donaldson–Thomas invariants are the Euler characteristics of the moduli spaces of stable objects, which in this setting are the dimensions of the spaces of conformal blocks. The wall‑crossing formula states that the product of quantum dilogarithms Φq(Xγ) over all charges γ in a given sector, ordered by the phase of the central charge Z(γ), is invariant under continuous deformation of the stability condition. Here Xγ are the quantum cluster variables associated to the charge γ, and q = exp(πi/(κ + h∨)). The invariance of this product is the mathematical incarnation of the physical statement that the spectrum of BPS states in the N = 2 theory on ℝ³ × S¹ is continuous across walls of marginal stability, with the discontinuities in the integer BPS indices compensated by the quantum dilogarithm factors. In the geometric Langlands correspondence, the wall‑crossing formula implies that the categorical trace of the monodromy of the quantum connection on the space of conformal blocks is independent of the choice of spectral curve. This monodromy is the quantum R‑matrix of the quantum group Uq(g), and its invariance under wall‑crossing is the quantum Yang–Baxter equation. The Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing formula for the Hitchin system was proved by Gaiotto, Moore, and Neitzke using the spectral network construction, which associates to each point in the Hitchin base a network of trajectories of the quadratic differential on the spectral curve. The spectral network computes the BPS indices and the quantum dilogarithm factors, and its combinatorics reproduces the cluster mutations of Fock and Goncharov. The wall‑crossing formula for D‑modules on BunG is the statement that the Fourier–Mukai transform along the Hitchin fibration intertwines the wall‑crossing automorphisms on the B‑model side with the cluster mutations on the A‑model side.

  • Confusing the parameter t in the Kapustin–Witten twist with the level κ: t = i(κ + h∨)−1/2; t = 0 corresponds to κ → ∞ (the classical limit of the quantum group), while t = ∞ corresponds to κ = −h∨ (the critical level). The quantum parameter is q = exp(πi/(κ + h∨)) = exp(−πt²).
  • Misidentifying the surface defect functor: For a defect (H, ρ), the functor is pullback along Bun_H → Bun_G followed by tensoring with the D‑module on Bun_H corresponding to ρ under geometric Satake. It is not simply the restriction of D‑modules to the substack of H‑reductions.
  • Treating Verlinde line operators as ordinary Hecke functors at non‑critical level: At κ ≠ −h∨, the Hecke functors are deformed by the quantum group U_q(g); their fusion is the truncated tensor product at root of unity, not the ordinary tensor product of representations of G.
  • Overlooking the quantum dilogarithm in cluster mutations: The classical cluster mutation x_k' = x_k⁻¹ ∏ x_i[εki]_+ is quantised to the conjugation by Φ_q(X_k); the pentagon identity for Φ_q is the quantum analogue of the mutation invariance.
  • Assuming wall‑crossing only affects numerical invariants: The Kontsevich–Soibelman formula is a categorical statement: the autoequivalence of the 3d Calabi–Yau category given by the product of spherical twists (or quantum dilogarithms) is independent of the stability condition. This categorical invariance underlies the equivalence of the geometric Langlands correspondence for different choices of spectral curve.
  • Confusing the Fock–Goncharov cluster variety with the Hitchin base: The cluster variety is LocSys_G (or its open part), while the Hitchin base is the space of spectral curves. The cluster coordinates are functions on LocSys_G, not on the Hitchin base. The quantum Hitchin Hamiltonians are the quantum cluster variables, and their eigenvalues are the quantum opers.
  • Neglecting the role of the Nahm pole in the Kapustin–Witten derivation: The Nahm pole boundary condition at the origin of the cigar is what forces the Hecke eigensheaf property; without it, the Kapustin–Witten equations only give a correspondence between boundary conditions, not the specific eigenvalue condition.

D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Group G₂ and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The exceptional group G₂ admits a 7‑dimensional fundamental representation that yields a distinguished Hitchin base of dimension 3g−3 for a curve of genus g. The corresponding spectral curves are 7‑fold covers of the base curve, and their Prym varieties carry a natural action of the Weyl group of G₂. The geometric Langlands correspondence for G₂ identifies the derived category of D‑modules on BunG₂ with the derived category of coherent sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysG₂ of G₂‑local systems. The Hecke eigensheaf condition is encoded by a family of Hecke functors whose composition rules reflect the tensor product of the 7‑dimensional representation with itself, which decomposes as 7 ⊗ 7 = 1 ⊕ 14 ⊕ 27. The 14‑dimensional adjoint representation governs the deformation of the Hitchin Hamiltonians, while the 27‑dimensional representation appears in the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence at non‑critical level. The categorical trace of the monodromy of the quantum connection on the space of conformal blocks yields the quantum R‑matrix of U_q(g₂), and its invariance under wall‑crossing in the Hitchin base is equivalent to the quantum Yang–Baxter equation for g₂. The spectral network construction of Gaiotto–Moore–Neitzke computes the BPS indices for the G₂ Hitchin system; the associated cluster mutations are quantised by the quantum dilogarithm Φ_q with q = exp(πi/(κ + h∨)), where h∨ = 4 for G₂. The wall‑crossing formula implies that the product of quantum dilogarithms over all charges in a sector, ordered by the phase of the central charge, is independent of the stability condition. This categorical invariance underlies the equivalence of the geometric Langlands correspondence for different choices of spectral curve.

D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Group F₄ and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

For the exceptional group F₄ the dual group is also F₄, so the geometric Langlands correspondence is self‑dual. The Hitchin base has dimension 15g−15, and the spectral curves are 24‑fold covers reflecting the 26‑dimensional fundamental representation (the trace‑free part of the 27‑dimensional Jordan algebra representation). The Hecke functors are built from the minuscule coweight corresponding to the 26‑dimensional representation; their fusion rules are governed by the tensor product 26 ⊗ 26 = 1 ⊕ 26 ⊕ 52 ⊕ 273 ⊕ 324. The 52‑dimensional adjoint representation controls the deformation of the Hitchin Hamiltonians. At the critical level κ = −h∨ (h∨ = 9 for F₄) the quantum parameter q = 1 and the Hecke category reduces to the ordinary Satake category of representations of F₄. At non‑critical level the Hecke functors are deformed by the quantum group U_q(F₄) and their fusion is the truncated tensor product at a root of unity. The quantum Hitchin Hamiltonians are the quantum cluster variables on the F₄ cluster variety, which coincides with an open part of LocSysF₄. The wall‑crossing formula for the F₄ Hitchin system involves the product of quantum dilogarithms Φ_q(X_γ) over all charges γ, with q = exp(πi/(κ+9)). The Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing automorphism is a categorical autoequivalence of the 3d Calabi–Yau category associated to the F₄ Hitchin system; its invariance under variation of the stability condition is the mathematical incarnation of the continuity of the BPS spectrum in the N = 2 theory on ℝ³ × S¹. The spectral network for F₄ is built from the quadratic differential on the 24‑fold spectral curve; its trajectories compute the BPS indices and the quantum dilogarithm factors, reproducing the F₄ cluster mutations of Fock–Goncharov.

Worked Example: Quantum Parameter for G₂ at Level κ
Compute the quantum parameter q for the G₂ Hitchin system at level κ = −2. Recall that the dual Coxeter number h∨ for G₂ is 4.
The quantum parameter is q = exp(πi/(κ + h∨)). Substituting κ = −2 and h∨ = 4 gives κ + h∨ = 2. Hence q = exp(πi/2) = i. Therefore the quantum parameter is i, a primitive fourth root of unity. At this level the quantum group U_i(g₂) is at a root of unity, and the Hecke functors are governed by the truncated tensor product of representations of U_i(g₂).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the quantum parameter q for an exceptional group, first recall the dual Coxeter number h∨ (G₂:4, F₄:9, E₆:12, E₇:18, E₈:30). Then apply q = exp(πi/(κ + h∨)). Remember that the critical level is κ = −h∨, where q = 1 and the quantum group becomes classical. For non‑critical levels, q is a root of unity precisely when κ + h∨ is a rational number with denominator dividing the order of the root of unity. Memorise the table of h∨ for the five exceptional groups; it is a standard fact that appears in many geometric Langlands problems.

D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Exceptional Group E₇ and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The exceptional group E₇ has dual Coxeter number h∨ = 18 and a 56‑dimensional fundamental representation (the symplectic representation). The Hitchin base for E₇ has dimension 63g−63, and the spectral curves are 56‑fold covers. The Hecke functors are attached to the minuscule coweight of the 56‑dimensional representation; their fusion rules follow the tensor product 56 ⊗ 56 = 1 ⊕ 133 ⊕ 1463 ⊕ 1539 ⊕ 6480. The 133‑dimensional adjoint representation governs the deformation of the Hitchin Hamiltonians. At the critical level κ = −18 the quantum parameter q = 1 and the Hecke category is the ordinary Satake category of representations of E₇. At non‑critical level the Hecke functors are deformed by U_q(E₇) with q = exp(πi/(κ+18)). The quantum Hitchin Hamiltonians are the quantum cluster variables on the E₇ cluster variety, an open part of LocSysE₇. The wall‑crossing formula for the E₇ Hitchin system involves the product of quantum dilogarithms Φ_q(X_γ) over all charges γ, ordered by the phase of the central charge Z(γ). The Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing automorphism is a categorical autoequivalence of the 3d Calabi–Yau category associated to the E₇ Hitchin system; its invariance under variation of the stability condition is equivalent to the quantum Yang–Baxter equation for the quantum R‑matrix of U_q(E₇). The spectral network for E₇ is built from the quadratic differential on the 56‑fold spectral curve; its trajectories compute the BPS indices and the quantum dilogarithm factors, reproducing the E₇ cluster mutations of Fock–Goncharov. The categorical trace of the monodromy of the quantum connection on the space of conformal blocks yields the quantum R‑matrix, and its wall‑crossing invariance implies the equivalence of the geometric Langlands correspondence for different choices of spectral curve.

D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Exceptional Group E₈ and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

E₈ is the largest exceptional group with dual Coxeter number h∨ = 30 and no non‑trivial minuscule representations; the smallest fundamental representation is the 248‑dimensional adjoint representation. Consequently the Hecke functors for E₈ are built from the adjoint coweight, and their fusion rules are governed by the tensor product 248 ⊗ 248 = 1 ⊕ 248 ⊕ 3875 ⊕ 27000 ⊕ 30380. The Hitchin base has dimension 120g−120, and the spectral curves are 248‑fold covers. At the critical level κ = −30 the quantum parameter q = 1 and the Hecke category reduces to the Satake category of representations of E₈. At non‑critical level the Hecke functors are deformed by U_q(E₈) with q = exp(πi/(κ+30)). The quantum Hitchin Hamiltonians are the quantum cluster variables on the E₈ cluster variety, which is an open part of LocSysE₈. The wall‑crossing formula for the E₈ Hitchin system involves the product of quantum dilogarithms Φ_q(X_γ) over all charges γ, with q = exp(πi/(κ+30)). The Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing automorphism is a categorical autoequivalence of the 3d Calabi–Yau category associated to the E₈ Hitchin system; its invariance under variation of the stability condition is the mathematical incarnation of the continuity of the BPS spectrum in the N = 2 theory on ℝ³ × S¹. The spectral network for E₈ is built from the quadratic differential on the 248‑fold spectral curve; its trajectories compute the BPS indices and the quantum dilogarithm factors, reproducing the E₈ cluster mutations of Fock–Goncharov. The categorical trace of the monodromy of the quantum connection on the space of conformal blocks yields the quantum R‑matrix of U_q(E₈), and its wall‑crossing invariance implies the equivalence of the geometric Langlands correspondence for different choices of spectral curve.

D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Group G₂ and the Parabolic Geometric Langlands Correspondence

When parabolic structures are introduced at marked points on the curve, the moduli stack BunG₂par parametrises G₂‑bundles with a reduction to a parabolic subgroup at each marked point. The corresponding moduli of local systems LocSysG₂par carries a natural symplectic structure, and the parabolic Hitchin base includes additional parameters encoding the residues of the Higgs field at the punctures. The Hecke functors are modified by the insertion of surface defect operators labelled by representations of the Levi subgroup of the parabolic. For G₂ the maximal parabolic subgroups correspond to the two nodes of the Dynkin diagram; their Levi factors are GL(1) × SL(2) and GL(1) × SL(3) respectively. The surface defect functor for a defect (H, ρ) is the pullback along Bun_H → BunG₂ followed by tensoring with the D‑module on Bun_H corresponding to ρ under geometric Satake. The parabolic Riemann–Hilbert correspondence identifies the category of parabolic D‑modules on BunG₂par with the category of constructible sheaves on LocSysG₂par with prescribed monodromy at the punctures. The wall‑crossing formula in the parabolic setting involves quantum dilogarithms associated to charges that include the parabolic weights; the product of these quantum dilogarithms, ordered by the phase of the extended central charge, is invariant under variation of the parabolic stability condition. This invariance underlies the equivalence of the parabolic geometric Langlands correspondence for different choices of parabolic spectral curve.

  • Confusing the dual Coxeter number h∨ for exceptional groups: G₂=4, F₄=9, E₆=12, E₇=18, E₈=30. Using the wrong h∨ gives an incorrect quantum parameter q.
  • Misidentifying the minuscule representation for a group: G₂ has a 7‑dimensional minuscule, F₄ a 26‑dimensional, E₆ a 27‑dimensional, E₇ a 56‑dimensional, while E₈ has none. Hecke functors are built from the minuscule coweight when it exists.
  • Treating the quantum parameter q = exp(πi/(κ+h∨)) as a formal variable without checking whether κ+h∨ is rational; at roots of unity the quantum group representation theory truncates.
  • Overlooking the role of the Nahm pole boundary condition in the Kapustin–Witten derivation for exceptional groups; without it the Hecke eigensheaf property is not forced.
  • Assuming the cluster variety for an exceptional group is the Hitchin base; the cluster variety is LocSys_G (or an open part), while the Hitchin base is the space of spectral curves.
  • Neglecting the parabolic weights in the wall‑crossing formula for parabolic Hitchin systems; the central charge Z(γ) must include the parabolic contributions.
  • Confusing the quantum dilogarithm Φ_q with the classical dilogarithm; the pentagon identity for Φ_q is the quantum analogue of mutation invariance and is essential for the wall‑crossing automorphism.

The Kashiwara–Schapira Index Theorem for Holonomic D‑modules on Singular Varieties

The Kashiwara–Schapira index theorem extends the classical Euler–Poincaré characteristic to the setting of holonomic D‑modules on possibly singular algebraic varieties. Let X be a complex algebraic variety (not necessarily smooth) and let M be a holonomic D‑module on X. The theorem asserts that the alternating sum of the dimensions of the solution complexes of M, taken with respect to a suitable stratification, equals the Euler characteristic of the characteristic cycle of M intersected with the zero section of the cotangent bundle. More precisely, if SS(M) denotes the characteristic variety of M, which is a Lagrangian conical subset of T*X, and CC(M) its characteristic cycle, then the index ind(M) = Σ (−1)^i dim H^i(X, Sol(M)) satisfies ind(M) = ∫T*X CC(M) ∧ [X], where [X] is the fundamental class of the zero section. The proof uses the microlocal theory of sheaves developed by Kashiwara and Schapira, in particular the construction of the microsupport of a constructible sheaf and the functoriality of the characteristic cycle under proper pushforward and smooth pullback. A key ingredient is the Kashiwara constructibility theorem, which guarantees that the solution complex Sol(M) is constructible with respect to a Whitney stratification adapted to SS(M). The index theorem has important consequences for the computation of Euler characteristics of intersection cohomology complexes on singular varieties, because the intersection cohomology D‑module IC_X is holonomic and its characteristic cycle is the conormal cycle of the stratification. In practice, the theorem reduces the calculation of topological invariants of singular spaces to a combinatorial problem on the characteristic cycle, which can often be read off from the geometry of the singular locus.

The Enhanced de Rham Functor and the Categorical Riemann–Hilbert Correspondence for Irregular Connections

The enhanced de Rham functor, introduced by D’Agnolo and Kashiwara, provides a refinement of the classical de Rham functor that captures the full Stokes data of irregular holonomic D‑modules. For a smooth complex variety X and a divisor D with normal crossings, the enhanced de Rham functor DR^E: D^b_hol(D_X(*D)) → E^b(I_X|X×ℝ) lands in the derived category of enhanced ind‑sheaves on the real blow‑up of X along D. The functor is constructed by first taking the usual de Rham complex of a D‑module with meromorphic poles along D, then enriching it with a filtration indexed by the real line that records the exponential growth rates of solutions in sectors around the singularities. The categorical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections states that DR^E induces an equivalence of triangulated categories between the bounded derived category of holonomic D‑modules with irregular singularities along D and the bounded derived category of constructible enhanced ind‑sheaves on the real blow‑up. The quasi‑inverse is given by the enhanced solution functor Sol^E. This equivalence respects the six operations (pushforward, pullback, tensor product, duality) and intertwines the irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence of Malgrange and Sibuya with the microlocal theory of enhanced sheaves. A concrete consequence is that the Stokes matrices of an irregular connection can be recovered as the gluing data of the corresponding enhanced sheaf across the sectors of the real blow‑up. The theory has been instrumental in proving the geometric Langlands correspondence for wild ramification, where the Hecke eigensheaf condition is formulated in terms of enhanced ind‑sheaves on the moduli stack of local systems with irregular singularities.

Worked Example: Computing the Index of a Holonomic D‑module on a Nodal Curve
Let X be the nodal cubic curve defined by y2 = x3 + x2 in ℂ2, and let M = O_X be the structure sheaf viewed as a D‑module via the trivial connection. Compute the Kashiwara–Schapira index ind(M).
The nodal cubic X has a single singular point at the origin. The structure sheaf O_X is holonomic because X is a curve. Its characteristic variety SS(O_X) is the zero section of T*X together with the conormal bundle to the singular point, which is a line in the cotangent space at the node. The characteristic cycle CC(O_X) = [T*_X X] + [T*node X]. The zero section [X] intersects the zero section component with multiplicity 1, and intersects the conormal component trivially because the conormal is disjoint from the zero section away from the node. Hence ∫T*X CC(O_X) ∧ [X] = 1. By the index theorem, ind(O_X) = 1. This matches the topological Euler characteristic of the normalization ℙ1 (which is 2) minus the contribution of the node (which is 1), giving 1.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the index of a holonomic D‑module on a singular variety, first identify a stratification for which the D‑module is constructible. Write down the characteristic cycle as a sum of conormal bundles to strata. The index is then the sum of the coefficients of the zero‑section components. This reduces a potentially heavy cohomology computation to a simple combinatorial count.

The Categorical Riemann–Hilbert Correspondence for Irregular Connections and Stokes Data

The categorical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections refines the classical equivalence between regular holonomic D‑modules and constructible sheaves by incorporating the Stokes phenomenon. Let X be a smooth complex variety and D a divisor with normal crossings. Consider the category Hol_irr(X,D) of holonomic D‑modules on X with irregular singularities along D, and the category Sh^E_ℝ(X,D) of enhanced ind‑sheaves on the real oriented blow‑up X̃ of X along D, constructible with respect to a stratification induced by the Stokes sectors. The enhanced de Rham functor DR^E: D^b(Hol_irr(X,D)) → D^b(Sh^E_ℝ(X,D)) is an equivalence of triangulated categories. Its quasi‑inverse is the enhanced solution functor Sol^E. The correspondence is compatible with the six functor formalism: for a morphism f: X → Y compatible with the divisors, the natural transformations DR^E ∘ f_* ≅ f_* ∘ DR^E and DR^E ∘ f^! ≅ f^! ∘ DR^E hold. Moreover, the duality functor D on D‑modules corresponds to the Verdier duality on enhanced sheaves twisted by the orientation sheaf of the real blow‑up. The Stokes data of an irregular connection appear as the gluing isomorphisms between the restrictions of the enhanced sheaf to adjacent sectors. In particular, the Stokes matrices are the transition maps of the local system of rapid decay solutions across the Stokes lines. This categorical framework allows one to transport geometric constructions from the D‑module side (e.g., Hecke modifications, convolution) to the sheaf side, where they become more accessible for the geometric Langlands program with wild ramification.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Group G₂ and the Parabolic Geometric Langlands Correspondence with Wild Ramification

Let C be a smooth projective curve over ℂ and let G be the exceptional group of type G₂. The moduli stack BunG₂par of parabolic G₂‑bundles on C with fixed parabolic weights at a finite set of marked points carries a natural action of the loop group LG₂. The category of D‑modules on BunG₂par that are equivariant with respect to a suitable level κ of the affine Kac–Moody algebra is expected to be equivalent, via a parabolic version of the geometric Langlands correspondence, to the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysG₂par, wild of parabolic local systems with wild ramification at the marked points. The wild ramification data are encoded by irregular singularities of the underlying connection, whose formal type is determined by a choice of irregular type in the sense of Boalch. The Hecke functors for G₂ are constructed from the minuscule coweight corresponding to the 7‑dimensional fundamental representation; they act on D‑modules by convolution with the Hecke eigensheaf on the affine Grassmannian GrG₂. At the critical level κ = −h^∨ = −4, the quantum parameter q = 1 and the Hecke category reduces to the Satake category of finite‑dimensional representations of G₂. For non‑critical levels, the Hecke functors are deformed by the quantum group U_q(G₂) with q = exp(πi/(κ+4)). The parabolic Riemann–Hilbert correspondence identifies the category of parabolic D‑modules on BunG₂par with the category of constructible sheaves on LocSysG₂par, wild having prescribed monodromy and Stokes data at the punctures. The wall‑crossing formula for the parabolic Hitchin system involves a product of quantum dilogarithms Φ_q(X_γ) over charges γ that include the parabolic weights; the ordering is by the phase of the extended central charge Z(γ). Invariance of this product under variation of the stability condition yields the equivalence of the parabolic geometric Langlands correspondence for different choices of parabolic spectral curve. This picture has been verified in the case of G₂ by explicit computation of the quantum Hitchin Hamiltonians and their relation to the cluster algebra structure on the G₂ character variety.

  • Trap 1: Confusing the dual Coxeter number h^∨ for G₂ (which is 4) with that of other exceptional groups; using the wrong value gives an incorrect quantum parameter q.
  • Trap 2: Assuming the minuscule representation of G₂ is the 14‑dimensional adjoint representation; the minuscule representation is the 7‑dimensional fundamental representation.
  • Trap 3: Treating the enhanced de Rham functor as the ordinary de Rham functor when irregular singularities are present; the enhanced version is essential to capture Stokes data.
  • Trap 4: Forgetting that the characteristic cycle of a holonomic D‑module on a singular variety may contain components supported on the conormal bundles to singular strata, which contribute to the index.
  • Trap 5: Overlooking the role of the real oriented blow‑up in the categorical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections; the equivalence lives on the blow‑up, not on the original variety.
  • Trap 6: Misidentifying the wall‑crossing product for parabolic Hitchin systems by omitting the parabolic weights in the central charge Z(γ).
  • Trap 7: Confusing the quantum dilogarithm Φ_q with the classical dilogarithm; the pentagon identity for Φ_q is the quantum analogue of mutation invariance and is crucial for the wall‑crossing automorphism.

These advanced topics illustrate how microlocal analysis, enhanced sheaf theory, and quantum group deformations intertwine in the modern theory of D‑modules and the geometric Langlands program.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles for the Exceptional Group E7 at the Critical Level

The critical level for an affine Kac–Moody algebra is defined by κ = −h^∨, where h^∨ is the dual Coxeter number. For the exceptional group E7 the dual Coxeter number equals 18, so the critical level is κ = −18. At this level the quantum parameter q = exp(πi/(κ+h^∨)) becomes q = 1, and the representation theory of the affine algebra collapses to the representation theory of the finite‑dimensional Langlands dual group, which for E7 is again E7. The category of D‑modules on the moduli stack BunE7 that are equivariant with respect to the critical‑level action of the loop group LE7 is expected to be equivalent, via the geometric Langlands correspondence, to the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysE7 of E7‑local systems on the curve. The Hecke functors are constructed from the minuscule coweights of E7; the fundamental representation of dimension 56 provides the minuscule weight, and the corresponding Hecke eigensheaf lives on the affine Grassmannian GrE7. At the critical level the Hecke category reduces to the Satake category of finite‑dimensional representations of E7, and the convolution product matches the tensor product of representations. The center of the completed universal enveloping algebra at the critical level is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of opers for the Langlands dual group; this isomorphism, proved by Feigin–Frenkel, identifies the spectral parameters of the quantum Hitchin Hamiltonians with the opers. The Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for regular singular connections identifies the de Rham functor DR from D‑modules to constructible sheaves with the solution functor; at the critical level the irregular part is absent, so the ordinary de Rham functor suffices. The decomposition theorem for holonomic D‑modules implies that the pushforward of the constant D‑module along the Hitchin fibration splits into a direct sum of intersection cohomology D‑modules, each corresponding to a perverse sheaf on the base. This splitting underlies the geometric Langlands correspondence for E7 at the critical level and provides a categorical framework for the endoscopic transfer of automorphic forms.

Examiner traps often involve confusing the dual Coxeter number of E7 (18) with that of E6 (12) or E8 (30), which leads to an incorrect critical level and quantum parameter. Another common error is assuming that the minuscule representation of E7 is the adjoint representation of dimension 133; the minuscule representation is the 56‑dimensional fundamental representation. Finally, students sometimes treat the critical‑level Hecke category as a deformation of the Satake category, whereas at κ = −h^∨ the deformation parameter q equals 1 and the category is exactly the Satake category.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles for the Exceptional Group E8 and the Quantum Geometric Langlands Correspondence

For the exceptional group E8 the dual Coxeter number is h^∨ = 30. The quantum geometric Langlands correspondence introduces a deformation parameter q = exp(πi/(κ+30)), where κ is the level of the affine Kac–Moody algebra. When κ is generic (non‑critical), q is a non‑trivial root of unity or a generic complex number, and the Hecke category is a braided monoidal category equivalent to the representation category of the quantum group U_q(E8). The moduli stack BunE8 carries a natural action of the loop group LE8, and the category of D‑modules on BunE8 equivariant with respect to the level‑κ action is conjecturally equivalent to the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysE8 of E8‑local systems equipped with a q‑difference structure. The Hecke functors are built from the minuscule coweights of E8; however E8 has no minuscule fundamental weights, so the Hecke operators are constructed from the adjoint representation (dimension 248) and from the fundamental representation of dimension 3875, which is quasi‑minuscule. The quantum parameter q controls the braiding in the Hecke category and appears in the R‑matrix of the quantum group. The quantum Hitchin system for E8 is obtained by quantizing the classical Hitchin Hamiltonians; the quantum Hamiltonians generate a commutative subalgebra of the quantum group and their joint eigenvalues are given by the opers for the Langlands dual group, which for E8 is again E8. The quantum Langlands correspondence predicts an equivalence between the category of D‑modules on BunE8 twisted by the quantum parameter and the category of q‑difference modules on the space of opers. The categorical trace of the Hecke functors yields characters of the quantum group, which match the trace functions of the corresponding automorphic sheaves. This picture has been verified in the case of the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence for GL(n) and is expected to extend to E8 via the Beilinson–Drinfeld quantization of the Hitchin system.

Typical pitfalls include using the dual Coxeter number of E7 (18) instead of 30 when computing q, forgetting that E8 lacks minuscule weights and therefore the Hecke functors are not given by a single fundamental representation, and confusing the quantum parameter q with the classical parameter ℏ in deformation quantization. Another trap is assuming that the quantum geometric Langlands correspondence for E8 is a straightforward deformation of the classical correspondence; the absence of minuscule weights forces the use of higher‑rank Hecke operators and modifies the Satake equivalence.

Identifying the quantum parameter
Given the dual Coxeter number h^∨ = 30 for E8, compute the quantum parameter q at level κ = −h^∨ + 1.
At level κ = −30 + 1 = −29 the denominator κ + h^∨ equals −29 + 30 = 1. Hence q = exp(πi / 1) = exp(πi) = −1. The quantum parameter is −1, a primitive second root of unity.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the dual Coxeter numbers for the exceptional groups (E6:12, E7:18, E8:30, F4:9, G2:4). When a question asks for the quantum parameter, first compute κ + h^∨, then evaluate exp(πi/(κ+h^∨)). Remember that at the critical level κ = −h^∨ the denominator vanishes, so q = 1 by convention. For non‑critical levels, the denominator is a non‑zero integer, giving a root of unity. Write the steps explicitly to avoid arithmetic slips.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles for the Group G2 and the Parabolic Geometric Langlands Correspondence with Wild Ramification

The exceptional group G2 has dual Coxeter number h^∨ = 4. In the parabolic setting one fixes a finite set of marked points on the curve C and prescribes parabolic weights for the G2‑bundle at those points. The moduli stack BunG2par of parabolic G2‑bundles carries an action of the loop group LG2, and the category of D‑modules on this stack equivariant at level κ is expected to correspond, via the parabolic geometric Langlands correspondence, to the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysG2par,wild of parabolic local systems with wild ramification. Wild ramification is encoded by irregular singularities of the underlying connection; the formal type of the irregularity is determined by an irregular type in the sense of Boalch, which for G2 can be taken from the 7‑dimensional minuscule representation. The Hecke functors for G2 are constructed from the minuscule coweight corresponding to the 7‑dimensional fundamental representation; they act by convolution with the Hecke eigensheaf on the affine Grassmannian GrG2. At the critical level κ = −4 the quantum parameter q = 1 and the Hecke category reduces to the Satake category of finite‑dimensional representations of G2. For non‑critical levels the Hecke functors are deformed by the quantum group U_q(G2) with q = exp(πi/(κ+4)). The parabolic Riemann–Hilbert correspondence identifies the category of parabolic D‑modules on BunG2par with the category of constructible sheaves on LocSysG2par,wild having prescribed monodromy and Stokes data at the punctures. The wall‑crossing formula for the parabolic Hitchin system involves a product of quantum dilogarithms Φ_q(X_γ) over charges γ that include the parabolic weights; the ordering is by the phase of the extended central charge Z(γ). Invariance of this product under variation of the stability condition yields the equivalence of the parabolic geometric Langlands correspondence for different choices of parabolic spectral curve. This picture has been verified in the case of G2 by explicit computation of the quantum Hitchin Hamiltonians and their relation to the cluster algebra structure on the G2 character variety.

Common traps: confusing the dual Coxeter number of G2 (4) with that of other exceptional groups; assuming the minuscule representation of G2 is the 14‑dimensional adjoint representation (it is the 7‑dimensional fundamental); treating the enhanced de Rham functor as the ordinary de Rham functor when irregular singularities are present; forgetting that the characteristic cycle of a holonomic D‑module on a singular variety may contain components supported on conormal bundles to singular strata; overlooking the role of the real oriented blow‑up in the categorical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles for the Group F4 and the Categorical Geometric Langlands Correspondence via the Whittaker Category

The exceptional group F4 has dual Coxeter number h^∨ = 9. The categorical geometric Langlands correspondence for F4 can be approached through the Whittaker category, which is the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack BunF4 equipped with a Whittaker condition at a chosen generic point of the curve. The Whittaker condition picks out a non‑degenerate character of the nilpotent radical of a Borel subgroup, and the resulting category is expected to be equivalent to the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysF4 with a compatible Whittaker structure. The Hecke functors for F4 are built from the minuscule coweights; F4 has two minuscule fundamental weights corresponding to the 26‑dimensional and 52‑dimensional representations. The quantum parameter at level κ is q = exp(πi/(κ+9)). At the critical level κ = −9 the quantum parameter equals 1 and the Hecke category becomes the Satake category of finite‑dimensional representations of F4. The center of the affine Kac–Moody algebra at the critical level is identified with the algebra of functions on the space of F4‑opers, and the quantum Hitchin Hamiltonians generate a commutative subalgebra acting on the Whittaker category. The categorical trace of the Hecke functors yields characters of the quantum group U_q(F4), which match the trace functions of the corresponding automorphic sheaves. The Whittaker normalization of the Hecke eigensheaves provides a canonical basis that is compatible with the cluster algebra structure on the F4 character variety. This framework has been used to prove cases of the geometric Langlands correspondence for F4 by reducing to the study of the quantum group and its representation theory.

Examiner traps: using the dual Coxeter number of E6 (12) instead of 9 for F4; assuming F4 has only one minuscule representation (it has two); confusing the Whittaker category with the full category of D‑modules on BunF4 (the Whittaker condition is a strong constraint); forgetting that the quantum parameter at the critical level is 1, not a non‑trivial root of unity; neglecting the role of the cluster algebra structure in organizing the Hecke eigensheaves.

D-modules on the Moduli Stack of G-Bundles for the Exceptional Group E6 and the Categorical Geometric Langlands Correspondence at the Critical Level

The exceptional group E6 has dual Coxeter number h^∨ = 12. At the critical level κ = −12 the quantum parameter q = 1, and the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence for E6 predicts an equivalence between the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack BunE6 equivariant under the critical‑level action of the loop group LE6 and the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysE6 of E6‑local systems. The Langlands dual of E6 is again E6, so the correspondence is self‑dual. The Hecke functors are constructed from the minuscule coweights of E6; there are two minuscule fundamental weights corresponding to the 27‑dimensional and its dual 27‑dimensional representations. The Hecke category at the critical level is the Satake category of finite‑dimensional representations of E6, and the convolution product matches the tensor product. The center of the completed universal enveloping algebra at the critical level is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of E6‑opers, as proved by Feigin–Frenkel. The quantum Hitchin system for E6 at the critical level reduces to the classical Hitchin system, and the Hitchin Hamiltonians act diagonally on the Hecke eigensheaves. The decomposition theorem for the Hitchin fibration yields a direct sum decomposition of the pushforward of the constant D‑module into intersection cohomology D‑modules, each corresponding to a perverse sheaf on the base. This decomposition underlies the geometric Langlands correspondence for E6 at the critical level and provides a categorical framework for the endoscopic transfer of automorphic forms. The categorical trace of the Hecke functors recovers the characters of the finite‑dimensional representations of E6, which match the trace functions of the corresponding automorphic sheaves.

Typical pitfalls: confusing the dual Coxeter number of E6 (12) with that of E7 (18) or E8 (30); assuming E6 has only one minuscule representation (it has two dual 27‑dimensional representations); treating the critical‑level Hecke category as a deformation of the Satake category (at κ = −h^∨ the deformation parameter q = 1, so the category is exactly the Satake category); forgetting that the Feigin–Frenkel isomorphism identifies the center with functions on opers, not with the whole algebra of differential operators; overlooking the role of the decomposition theorem in splitting the pushforward along the Hitchin fibration.

  • Trap 1: Using the wrong dual Coxeter number for an exceptional group leads to an incorrect critical level and quantum parameter.
  • Trap 2: Assuming a group has a single minuscule representation when it has two (E6, F4) or none (E8).
  • Trap 3: Confusing the ordinary de Rham functor with the enhanced de Rham functor in the presence of irregular singularities.
  • Trap 4: Forgetting that the characteristic cycle on a singular variety may contain components supported on conormal bundles to singular strata.
  • Trap 5: Overlooking the real oriented blow‑up in the categorical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections.
  • Trap 6: Misidentifying the wall‑crossing product for parabolic Hitchin systems by omitting parabolic weights in the central charge.
  • Trap 7: Confusing the quantum dilogarithm Φ_q with the classical dilogarithm; the pentagon identity for Φ_q is essential for wall‑crossing invariance.

This framework has been used to prove cases of the geometric Langlands correspondence for F₄ by reducing to the study of the quantum group and its representation theory. Examiner traps: using the dual Coxeter number of E₆ (12) instead of 9 for F₄; assuming F₄ has only one minuscule representation (it has two); confusing the Whittaker category with the full category of D‑modules on BunF₄ (the Whittaker condition is a strong constraint); forgetting that the quantum parameter at the critical level is 1, not a non‑trivial root of unity; neglecting the role of the cluster algebra structure in organizing the Hecke eigensheaves.

Worked Example: Critical Level for G₂
Compute the critical level κ for the exceptional group G₂ and state the resulting quantum parameter q.
The dual Coxeter number of G₂ is h^∨ = 4. The critical level is κ = −h^∨ = −4. At this level the quantum parameter q = exp(π i / (κ + h^∨)) = exp(π i / 0) which is interpreted as q = 1 in the categorical setting. Therefore κ = −4 and q = 1.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the dual Coxeter numbers for the exceptional groups (G₂:4, F₄:9, E₆:12, E₇:18, E₈:30). When a question asks for the critical level, immediately write κ = −h^∨ and note that q = 1. This avoids the common trap of inserting a non‑trivial root of unity.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Exceptional Group E₆ and the Categorical Geometric Langlands Correspondence at the Critical Level

The exceptional group E₆ has dual Coxeter number h^∨ = 12. At the critical level κ = −12 the quantum parameter q = 1, and the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence for E₆ predicts an equivalence between the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack BunE₆ equivariant under the critical‑level action of the loop group LE₆ and the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysE₆ of E₆‑local systems. The Langlands dual of E₆ is again E₆, so the correspondence is self‑dual. The Hecke functors are constructed from the minuscule coweights of E₆; there are two minuscule fundamental weights corresponding to the 27‑dimensional and its dual 27‑dimensional representations. The Hecke category at the critical level is the Satake category of finite‑dimensional representations of E₆, and the convolution product matches the tensor product. The center of the completed universal enveloping algebra at the critical level is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of E₆‑opers, as proved by Feigin–Frenkel. The quantum Hitchin system for E₆ at the critical level reduces to the classical Hitchin system, and the Hitchin Hamiltonians act diagonally on the Hecke eigensheaves. The decomposition theorem for the Hitchin fibration yields a direct sum decomposition of the pushforward of the constant D‑module into intersection cohomology D‑modules, each corresponding to a perverse sheaf on the base. This decomposition underlies the geometric Langlands correspondence for E₆ at the critical level and provides a categorical framework for the endoscopic transfer of automorphic forms. The categorical trace of the Hecke functors recovers the characters of the finite‑dimensional representations of E₆, which match the trace functions of the corresponding automorphic sheaves. Typical pitfalls: confusing the dual Coxeter number of E₆ (12) with that of E₇ (18) or E₈ (30); assuming E₆ has only one minuscule representation (it has two dual 27‑dimensional representations); treating the critical‑level Hecke category as a deformation of the Satake category (at κ = −h^∨ the deformation parameter q = 1, so the category is exactly the Satake category); forgetting that the Feigin–Frenkel isomorphism identifies the center with functions on opers, not with the whole algebra of differential operators; overlooking the role of the decomposition theorem in splitting the pushforward along the Hitchin fibration.

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Exceptional Group E₇ and the Categorical Geometric Langlands Correspondence at the Critical Level

The Theory of D‑modules on the Moduli Stack of G‑Bundles for the Exceptional Group E₈ and the Categorical Geometric Langlands Correspondence at the Critical Level

The exceptional group E₈ has dual Coxeter number h^∨ = 30. At the critical level κ = −30 the quantum parameter q = 1, and the categorical geometric Langlands correspondence for E₈ predicts an equivalence between the category of D‑modules on the moduli stack BunE₈ equivariant under the critical‑level action of the loop group LE₈ and the category of constructible sheaves on the moduli stack LocSysE₈ of E₈‑local systems. The Langlands dual of E₈ is again E₈, so the correspondence is self‑dual. The Hecke functors are constructed from the minuscule coweights of E₈; E₈ has no minuscule fundamental weights, so the Hecke category is built from the adjoint representation and its tensor powers. The Hecke category at the critical level is the Satake category of finite‑dimensional representations of E₈, and the convolution product matches the tensor product. The center of the completed universal enveloping algebra at the critical level is isomorphic to the algebra of functions on the space of E₈‑opers, as proved by Feigin–Frenkel. The quantum Hitchin system for E₈ at the critical level reduces to the classical Hitchin system, and the Hitchin Hamiltonians act diagonally on the Hecke eigensheaves. The decomposition theorem for the Hitchin fibration yields a direct sum decomposition of the pushforward of the constant D‑module into intersection cohomology D‑modules, each corresponding to a perverse sheaf on the base. This decomposition underlies the geometric Langlands correspondence for E₈ at the critical level and provides a categorical framework for the endoscopic transfer of automorphic forms. The categorical trace of the Hecke functors recovers the characters of the finite‑dimensional representations of E₈, which match the trace functions of the corresponding automorphic sheaves. Typical pitfalls: confusing the dual Coxeter number of E₈ (30) with that of E₆ (12) or E₇ (18); assuming E₈ possesses a minuscule representation (it has none); treating the critical‑level Hecke category as a deformation of the Satake category (at κ = −h^∨ the deformation parameter q = 1, so the category is exactly the Satake category); forgetting that the Feigin–Frenkel isomorphism identifies the center with functions on opers, not with the whole algebra of differential operators; overlooking the role of the decomposition theorem in splitting the pushforward along the Hitchin fibration.

  • Trap 1: Using the wrong dual Coxeter number for an exceptional group leads to an incorrect critical level and quantum parameter.
  • Trap 2: Assuming a group has a single minuscule representation when it has two (E₆, F₄) or none (E₈).
  • Trap 3: Confusing the ordinary de Rham functor with the enhanced de Rham functor in the presence of irregular singularities.

  • Trap 5: Overlooking the real oriented blow‑up in the categorical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections.
  • Trap 6: Misidentifying the wall‑crossing product for parabolic Hitchin systems by omitting parabolic weights in the central charge.
  • Trap 7: Confusing the quantum dilogarithm Φ_q with the classical dilogarithm; the pentagon identity for Φ_q is essential for wall‑crossing invariance.
